High-Dimensional Low-Rank Tensor Autoregressive Time Series Modeling
Di Wang, Yao Zheng, Guodong Li

Abstract
Modern technological advances have enabled an unprecedented amount of structured data with complex temporal dependence, urging the need for new methods to efficiently model and forecast high-dimensional tensor-valued time series. This paper provides a new modeling framework to accomplish this task via autoregression (AR). By considering a low-rank Tucker decomposition for the transition tensor, the proposed tensor AR can flexibly capture the underlying low-dimensional tensor dynamics, providing both substantial dimension reduction and meaningful multi-dimensional dynamic factor interpretations. For this model, we first study several nuclear-norm-regularized estimation methods and derive their non-asymptotic properties under the approximate low-rank setting. In particular, by leveraging the special balanced structure of the transition tensor, a novel convex regularization approach based on the sum of nuclear norms of square matricizations is proposed to efficiently encourage low-rankness of the coefficient tensor. To further improve the estimation efficiency under exact low-rankness, a non-convex estimator is proposed with a gradient descent algorithm, and its computational and statistical convergence guarantees are established. Simulation studies and an empirical analysis of tensor-valued time series data from multi-category import-export networks demonstrate the advantages of the proposed approach.



a
School of Mathematical Sciences, Shanghai Jiao Tong University, China

b
Department of Statistics, University of Connecticut, United States of America

c
Department of Statistics and Actuarial Science, University of Hong Kong, China

Keywords
: global trade flows; high-dimensional time series; non-convex tensor regression; nuclear norm; tensor decomposition; tensor-valued time series

1
Introduction

The rapid improvement in data collection capability has enabled the generation of increasingly more comprehensive economic datasets. Meanwhile, significant progress has been made in unifying data collection standards. These advances have led to an abundance of comparable disaggregated time series datasets across countries, which are further categorized by various dimensions like regions, industries, goods categories, and demographics.
Such multidimensional datasets can often be organized as multi-way arrays, forming tensor-valued time series. Moreover, this type of detailed time series data is common in finance, where it can be formed, e.g., by categorizing stock returns based on various firm characteristics dimensions, or asset returns across asset classes, regions, and sectors.
The availability of extensive disaggregated data, in turn, provides new opportunities to advance techniques for modeling complex dynamic systems like the global economy and financial markets.

The motivation behind the study of tensor-valued time series stems from the modeling of temporal and cross-sectional dependencies in panel data. To illustrate, first consider the panel data
\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{y}_{1,t}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{N,t}^{\top})^{\top}
for
N
countries, where
1\leq t\leq T
represents time, and
\mathbf{y}_{i,t}
is a vector containing observations of different economic variables for country
i
with
1\leq i\leq N
. For example,

Bussière et al., 2012
models the international trade by fitting a Global Vector Autoregressive (GVAR) model
(
Pesaran et al., 2004
)
to
\mathbf{y}_{i,t}
which includes the aggregate export and import volumes of country
i
,
ex_{i,t}
and
im_{i,t}
, as key variables. Compared to previous methods,
Bussière et al., 2012
’s approach has two major strengths: (1) it captures interdependencies across countries, i.e. cross-country spillovers; (2) it jointly models exports and imports, allowing for co-movements between them, which is important as exporting firms typically import components.

However, aggregate-level exports and imports data are limited in providing a comprehensive understanding of global trade dynamics. They do not provide information about how exports from one country are distributed among importing countries. By contrast, a much more detailed perspective on the trade flows can be gained from the disaggregated data
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(ex_{i,j,t})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
, where
ex_{i,j,t}
represents exports from one specific country (country
i
) to another specific country (country
j
) for all possible pairs of countries. Note that
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
is an
N\times N
matrix-valued time series, and by convention
ex_{i,i,t}
’s are set to zero. Since
ex_{i,t}=\sum_{j\neq i}ex_{i,j,t}
and
im_{i,t}=\sum_{j\neq i}ex_{j,i,t}
, the disaggregated series
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
contains all information of the aggregate series. Furthermore, we can have an even more granular view by further breaking
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
down into additional dimensions. For instance, the data can be divided into
K
different product categories, resulting in the tensor-valued time series
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=(ex_{i,j,k,t})_{1\leq i,j\leq N,1\leq k\leq K}
, where
ex_{i,j,k,t}
represents exports of product category
k
from country
i
to country
j
; see Figure
1
for an illustration.

Figure 1:
Illustration of vector-, matrix- and tensor-valued time series
\mathbf{y}_{t}=(ex_{1,t},im_{1,t},\dots,ex_{N,t},im_{N,t})^{\top}
,
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(ex_{i,j,t})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=(ex_{i,j,k,t})_{1\leq i,j\leq N,1\leq k\leq K}
in the context of modeling international trade dynamics via import-export data, where
N
is the number of countries, and
K
is the number of product categories.

More broadly, this paper considers autoregressive modeling of a general tensor-valued time series
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
, where the total number of series
p:=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
can be much larger than
T
. A naïve method is to apply models for panel data to the vectorized series
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})
, such as the vector autoregressive (VAR) model,

\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})=\mathbf{A}\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})+\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}),

(1)

and then perform dimension reduction for the unknown transition matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
via generic regularization methods such as the Lasso
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Han et al., 2015
)
, or data-specific methods
(
Pesaran et al., 2004
;
Canova and Ciccarelli, 2013
;
Guo et al., 2016
;
Zhu et al., 2017
;
Zheng and Cheng, 2021
)
which impose parameter restrictions based on pre-determined network structures. However, the vectorization undermines the model interpretability that could have been a valuable advantage of multi-dimensional data. For example, it would be much easier to gain meaningful insights into the global trade flow from the multi-category import-export data
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times K}
mentioned above if patterns across exporting countries, importing countries, and product categories can be separately interpreted. In particular, adopting a multi-dimensional approach, as proposed in this paper, enables us to address the following questions, which cannot be answered using the vector model:

(i)

Among all countries, whose exporting activities are the driving forces of the global trade flow? Are there any geographical groupings among them?

(ii)

Similar to (i), what about the importing activities?

(iii)

Among all product categories, which ones are the driving forces of the global trade flow? Are there any grouping patterns?

(iv)

Do the past and present states of the dynamic system (i.e., predictor and response) have the same grouping patterns across exporting countries, importing countries, and product categories?

Specifically, for the tensor-valued time series
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
, this paper proposes the Low-Rank Tensor Autoregressive (LRTAR) model by folding the
p\times p
transition matrix
\mathbf{A}
in (
1
), with
p=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
, into the
2d
-th-order transition tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}\times p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
which is assumed to have Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
with
r_{i}
being possibly much smaller than
p_{i}
, where
p_{d+i}=p_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,d
. This implies the Tucker decomposition
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times r_{i}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{2d}}
, and consequently the low-dimensional structure of the process
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}
as follows:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\times_{i=d+1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\times_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\times_{i=d+1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top},

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\times_{i=d+1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\times_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}
can be viewed as
r_{d+1}\times\cdots\times r_{2d}
and
r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{d}
factors summarizing the dynamic information across all dimensions. Moreover, each loading matrix
\mathbf{U}_{i}
reveals interpretable patterns for a particular dimension of the present or past state of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}
; see Section
2.4
for more detailed descriptions in the context of import-export data. The proposed model has the following features:

•

Similar to panel data models, it captures both cross-sectional and temporal dependencies. However, by leveraging the tensor structure, it dissects the cross-sectional information into
d
different dimensions, allowing for separate interpretations in each dimension.

•

Simultaneous dimension reduction is achieved across all dimensions of the transition tensor
\mathscr{A}
via the low-Tucker-rank assumption. This approach does not rely on predetermined parameter restrictions derived from the user’s prior knowledge or beliefs about the network structure.

•

The low-Tucker-rank assumption implies that factors are extracted across all dimensions of the response and its lagged predictor. The factor loadings facilitate the discernment of patterns in each dimension (i.e., mode) of the tensor-valued observation.

For the proposed model, this paper introduces two types of high-dimensional estimation methods: (i) convex estimators via nuclear norm regularizations and (ii) the non-convex estimator.
For (i), we consider the general setting where the transition tensor
\mathscr{A}
is approximately low-rank, and develop convex estimation methods based on different nuclear norm regularizations. Firstly, to encourage low-rankness along all modes, we study the widely-used Sum of Nuclear (SN) norm regularizer, defined as the sum of nuclear norms of all one-mode matricizations. However, due to the
fat-and-short
shape of the one-mode matricizations, the SN regularized estimator suffers from serious efficiency loss and hence performs even worse than the conventional Matrix Nuclear (MN) norm regularized estimator
(
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
)
which simply penalizes the nuclear norm of the transition matrix
\mathbf{A}
in (
1
). Thus, we further introduce a novel Sum of Square-matrix Nuclear (SSN) norm regularizer, defined as the sum of nuclear norms of all
p\times p
square matricizations of
\mathscr{A}
. The SSN reguarlized estimator is provably more efficient than the SN regularized one; see Theorem
3
and the first simulation experiment in Section
5.1
. In addition, we propose a truncated variants of the SSN estimator and prove its rank selection consistency when
\mathscr{A}
is exactly low-Tucker-rank under mild conditions.

However, the consistency of the SSN estimator requires that
T
grows faster than
p=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
. Thus, it may not be applicable to high-dimensional tensor-valued time series datasets with large
p_{i}
’s. This motivates us to consider a non-convex estimation method to further improve the estimation efficiency and relax the sample size requirement. Specifically, under the assumption that
\mathscr{A}
is exactly low-rank, this paper develops an estimator via non-convex (NC) optimization based on the explicit Tucker decomposition structure. A gradient descent algorithm is proposed for the NC estimator, with rigorous statistical and computational convergence guarantees. Compared with the convex estimators via nuclear norm regularizations, the consistency of the NC estimator only requires that
T
grows faster than
\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{i}
, which makes it attractive under high dimensionality. Although this approach requires initial values and known tensor ranks, the ridge-type ratio estimator can be used for determination of tensor ranks and initialization of the gradient descent algorithm.

This work is related to the literature on matrix-variate regression and tensor regression for independent data. The matrix-variate regression in
Ding and Cook, 2018
has the same basic bilinear form, while an envelope method was introduced to further reduce the dimension.

Raskutti et al., 2019
proposed a multi-response tensor regression model, where they mainly studied the third-order coefficient tensor and the SN regularization which is known to be statistically sub-optimal for higher-order tensor estimation. By contrast, we study the model for general higher-order tensor-valued time series. Moreover, our SSN estimator has a much faster statistical convergence rate than the SN estimator. For the non-convex tensor estimation problem,
Chen et al., 2019
and
Han et al., 2022
studied non-convex projected gradient descent methods for tensor regression. Our NC estimator can be viewed as a higher-order extension of the estimation approach in
Han et al., 2022
.
In addition, existing literature on tensor regression has only considered independent data or Gaussian time series data, whereas we allow sub-Gaussianity of the time series. This is a non-trivial relaxation, since unlike the Gaussian case, sub-Gaussian time series cannot be linearly transformed into independent samples.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2.1
introduces basic notation and tensor algebra. Section
2.2
presents the proposed LRTAR model. A series of nuclear-norm-regularized estimation methods are covered in Section
3
, where we develop the non-asymptotic theory for three regularized estimators and rank selection consistency for the truncated estimator. Section
4
proposes a non-convex estimation approach and presents its computational guarantees and statistical efficiency improvement.
Section
5
presents simulation studies and a real data analysis. Section
6
concludes with a brief discussion. We provide all technical proofs, algorithms, and additional discussions in a separate online supplementary file.

2
Tensor Decomposition and Tensor Autoregression

2.1
Preliminaries: Notation and Tensor Algebra

Tensors, also known as multi-dimensional arrays, are natural higher-order extensions of matrices. The order of a tensor is known as the dimension, way or mode, so a multi-dimensional array
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
is called an
m
-th order tensor. We introduce some important notations and concepts of tensor operation in this subsection, and refer readers to
Kolda and Bader, 2009
for a detailed review of basic tensor algebra.

Throughout this paper, we denote vectors by boldface small letters, e.g.
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
, matrices by boldface capital letters, e.g.
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}
, and tensors by boldface Euler capital letters, e.g.
\mathscr{X}
,
\mathscr{Y}
. For any two real-valued sequences
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
, we write
x_{k}\gtrsim y_{k}
if there exists a constant
c>0
such that
x_{k}\geq cy_{k}
for all
k
, and write
x_{k}\gg y_{k}
if
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}y_{k}/x_{k}=0
. In addition, write
x_{k}\asymp y_{k}
if
x_{k}\gtrsim y_{k}
and
y_{k}\gtrsim x_{k}
. We use
C
to denote a generic positive constant, which is independent of the dimensions and the sample size.

For a generic matrix
\mathbf{X}
, we let
\mathbf{X}^{\top}
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{F}}
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{op}}
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{nuc}}
,
\text{vec}(\mathbf{X})
, and
\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{X})
denote its transpose, Frobenius norm, operator norm, nuclear norm, vectorization, and
j
-th largest singular value, respectively. For any matrix
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, recall that the nuclear norm and its dual norm, the operator norm, are defined as

\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{nuc}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\min(p,q)}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{X})~~\text{and}~~\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{op}}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{X}).

(2)

For any square matrix
\mathbf{X}
, we let
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X})
and
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
denote its minimum and maximum eigenvalues.
For any real symmetric matrices
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
, we write
\mathbf{X}\leq\mathbf{Y}
if
\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{X}
is a positive semidefinite matrix.

Matricization, also known as unfolding, is the process of reordering the elements of a third- or higher-order tensor into a matrix. The most commonly used matricization is the one-mode matricization defined as follows. For any
m
-th-order tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
, its mode-
s
matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(s)}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{s}\times p_{-s}}
, with
p_{-s}=\prod_{i=1,i\neq s}^{m}p_{i}
, is the matrix obtained by setting the
s
-th tensor mode as its rows and collapsing all the others into its columns, for
s=1,\dots,m
. Specifically, the
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
-th element of
\mathscr{X}
is mapped to the
(i_{s},j)
-th element of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(s)}
, where

j=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq s\end{subarray}}^{m}(i_{k}-1)J_{k}~~\text{with}~~J_{k}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\ell\neq s\end{subarray}}^{k-1}p_{\ell}.

(3)

The above one-mode matricization can be extended to the multi-mode matricization by combining multiple modes to rows and combining the rest to columns of a matrix. For any index subset
S\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
, the multi-mode matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[S]}
is the
\prod_{i\in S}p_{i}
-by-
\prod_{i\notin S}p_{i}
matrix whose
(i,j)
-th element is mapped from the
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
-th element of
\mathscr{X}
, where

i=1+\sum_{k\in S}(i_{k}-1)I_{k}~\text{and}~j=1+\sum_{k\notin S}(i_{k}-1)J_{k},~\text{with}~I_{k}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in S\\
\ell<k\end{subarray}}p_{\ell}~\text{and}~J_{k}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\notin S\\
\ell<k\end{subarray}}p_{\ell}.

(4)

Note that the modes in the multi-mode matricization are collapsed following their original order
1,\dots,m
.
Moreover, it holds
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[S]}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[S^{\complement}]}^{\top}
, where
S^{\complement}=\{1,\dots,m\}\setminus S
is the complement of
S
.
In addition, the one-mode matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(s)}
defined above is simply
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[\{s\}]}
.

We next review the concepts of tensor-matrix multiplication, tensor generalized inner product and norm. For any
m
-th-order tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
and matrix
\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{k}\times p_{k}}
with
1\leq k\leq m
,
the mode-
k
multiplication
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\times_{k}\mathbf{Y}
produces an
m
-th-order tensor in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{k-1}\times q_{k}\times p_{k+1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
defined by

\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\times_{k}\mathbf{Y}\right)_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k-1}ji_{k+1}\dots i_{d}}=\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{p_{k}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{m}}\mathbf{Y}_{ji_{k}}.

(5)

For any two tensors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}\times\cdots p_{n}}
with
m\geq n
, their generalized inner product
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\rangle
is the
(m-n)
-th-order tensor in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{n+1}\times\dots\times p_{m}}
defined by

\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\rangle_{i_{n+1}\dots i_{m}}=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{p_{1}}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{p_{2}}\dots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{p_{n}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}i_{n+1}\dots i_{m}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}},

(6)

where
1\leq i_{n+1}\leq p_{n+1},\dots,1\leq i_{m}\leq p_{m}
. In particular, when
m=n
, it reduces to the conventional real-valued inner product. In addition, the Frobenius norm of any tensor
\mathscr{X}
is defined as
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\|_{\text{F}}=\sqrt{\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\rangle}
.

Some basic properties of the tensor generalized inner product are as follows. Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}\times\cdots p_{n}}
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Z}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{k-1}\times q_{k}\times p_{k+1}\cdots\times p_{n}}
be tensors with
m\geq n\geq k\geq 1
. If
\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{k}\times p_{k}}
, then
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\times_{k}\mathbf{Y},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Z}$}\rangle=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Z}$}\times_{k}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}\rangle
.
If
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{n+j}\times p_{n+j}}
with
1\leq j\leq m-n
, then
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\rangle\times_{j}\mathbf{Z}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\times_{n+j}\mathbf{Z},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\rangle
. Moreover,

\text{vec}(\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\rangle)=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[S]}\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}),

(7)

where
S=\{n+1,\dots,m\}
, and when
m=n
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[\emptyset]}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$})^{\top}
.

Finally, we summarize some concepts and useful results of the Tucker decomposition
(
Tucker, 1966
;
De Lathauwer et al., 2000
)
. For any tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
, its Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})
are defined as the matrix ranks of its one-mode matricizations, namely
r_{i}=\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(i)})
, for
i=1,\dots,m
. Note that
r_{i}
’s are analogous to the row and column ranks of a matrix, but are not necessarily equal for third- and higher-order tensors. However, the Tucker ranks must satisfy the condition

\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}r_{i}\right)^{2}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}.

(8)

If only one of the
r_{i}
’s is equal to the maximum rank
r_{\max}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}r_{i}
, (
8
)
is equivalent to
r_{\max}\leq\prod_{i=1,r_{i}\neq r_{\max}}^{m}r_{i}
; that is, the maximum Tucker rank must be no greater than the product of the other ranks.

Suppose that
\mathscr{X}
has Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})
. Then
\mathscr{X}
has the following Tucker decomposition:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\times_{1}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\times_{2}\mathbf{Y}_{2}\cdots\times_{m}\mathbf{Y}_{m}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\times_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{Y}_{i},

(9)

where
\mathbf{Y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times r_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,m
are the factor matrices and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{m}}
is the core tensor.
If
\mathscr{X}
has the Tucker decomposition in (
9
), then we have the following results for its one- and multi-mode matricizations:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(s)}=\mathbf{Y}_{s}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{(s)}(\mathbf{Y}_{d}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{Y}_{s+1}\otimes\mathbf{Y}_{s-1}\cdots\otimes\mathbf{Y}_{1})^{\top}=\mathbf{Y}_{s}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{(s)}(\otimes_{i\neq s}\mathbf{Y}_{i})^{\top},\quad s=1,\dots,m,

(10)

and

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{[S]}=(\otimes_{i\in S}\mathbf{Y}_{i})\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{[S]}(\otimes_{i\notin S}\mathbf{Y}_{i})^{\top},\quad S\subset\{1,\dots,m\},

(11)

where
\otimes_{i\neq s},\otimes_{i\in S}
and
\otimes_{i\notin S}
are matrix Kronecker products operating in the reverse order within the corresponding index sets.

2.2
Low-Rank Tensor Autoregression

For the tensor-valued time series
\{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
, we propose the following Low-Rank Tensor Autoregressive (LRTAR) model:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t},

(12)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}\times p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
is the
2d
-th-order transition tensor which is assumed to have Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
with
r_{i}=\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})
,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the generalized tensor inner product defined in (
6
) with
m=2d
and
n=d
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
is the mean-zero random error at time
t
with possible dependencies among its contemporaneous elements.

By Section
2.1
,
\mathscr{A}
admits the following Tucker decomposition:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i},

(13)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{2d}}
is the core tensor, and
\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times r_{i}}
are factor matrices for
1\leq i\leq 2d
.
Note that for any nonsingular matrices
\mathbf{O}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{i}\times r_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,2d
, it holds

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{O}_{i})\times_{i=1}^{2d}(\mathbf{U}_{i}\mathbf{O}_{i}^{-1}).

Thus, although the coefficient tensor
\mathscr{A}
in (
12
) is identifiable, its Tucker decomposition in (
13
) suffers from rotational indeterminacy.
To pin down the rotation matrices
\mathbf{O}_{i}
’s, a special Tucker decomposition, called the higher-order singular value decomposition (HOSVD), is commonly considered
(
Kolda and Bader, 2009
)
.
In the HOSVD, the factor matrix
\mathbf{U}_{i}
is defined as the tall orthonormal matrix consisting of the top
r_{i}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}
, for
i=1,\dots,2d
. This further implies that the core tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}
has the all-orthogonal property as follows:
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}^{\top}
is a diagonal matrix for
i=1,\dots,2d
.
We will formally discuss the identification conditions of
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s in Section
2.3
.

Denote
S_{1}=\{1,2,\dots,d\}
and
S_{2}=\{d+1,d+2,\dots,2d\}
. Note that by (
7
), model (
12
) can be written into the VAR form in (
1
) with transition matrix
\mathbf{A}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{2}]}
, i.e.,

\underbrace{\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})}_{\mathbf{y}_{t}}=\underbrace{(\otimes_{i\in S_{2}}\mathbf{U}_{i})\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{[S_{2}]}(\otimes_{i\in S_{1}}\mathbf{U}_{i})^{\top}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}_{[S_{2}]}}\underbrace{\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})}_{\mathbf{y}_{t-1}}+\underbrace{\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})}_{\mathbf{e}_{t}},

(14)

where
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})
and
\mathbf{e}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})
.

By the VAR representation in (
14
), we immediately have the necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of a unique strictly stationary solution to model (
12
) as follows.

Assumption 1
.

The spectral radius of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{2}]}
is strictly less than one.

2.3
Model Identification

To measure the extent of dimension reduction for the parameter space through the low-Tucker-rank assumption on
\mathscr{A}
, it is necessary to rule out the rotational indeterminacy of the Tucker decomposition.
As mentioned in Section
2.2
, the HOSVD can be used to solve the rotational indeterminacy. Specifically, under the HOSVD, we have

\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}^{\top}\text{ is a diagonal matrix},

(15)

for
i=1,\dots,2d
. Thus, (
15
) provides a convenient way for us to compute the effective number of degrees of freedom for the proposed LRTAR model. Specifically, by subtracting the number of constraints induced by (
15
) from the total number of parameters in
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s, we can obtain that the effective number of degrees of freedom for model (
12
) is

\prod_{i=1}^{2d}r_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}r_{i}(p_{i}-r_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{d}r_{d+i}(p_{i}-r_{d+i}).

(16)

This is substantially smaller than the total number of parameters in
\mathscr{A}
, i.e.,
p^{2}
, with
p=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
. For the example with
d=3
and
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=20
, if
r_{1}=\cdots=r_{6}=2
, then the number of parameters will be reduced from
p^{2}=64,000,000
to 280.

While the HOSVD avoids the rotational indeterminacy, it is still not necessarily unique in general. It is possible, however, to guarantee the uniqueness of the HOSVD under the additional assumption that the singular values of each one-mode matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}
are distinct for
i=1,\dots,2d
.
Under this assumption, each
\mathbf{U}_{i}
contains the
r_{i}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}
corresponding to the largest
r_{i}
singular values which are all distinct. To further avoid the indeterminacy due to sign switches of the singular vectors, it suffices to require that the first nonzero element in each column of
\mathbf{U}_{i}
is positive. As a result, such an HOSVD will be unique.

However, despite the non-uniqueness of the Tucker decomposition, the transition tensor
\mathscr{A}
itself is uniquely defined. Thus, the identification problem will not be an issue for the estimation of the low-Tucker-rank tensor
\mathscr{A}
. Indeed, in Sections
3
and
4
, we will introduce two types of methods to estimate
\mathscr{A}
. None of them requires a unique Tucker decomposition of
\mathscr{A}
. In practice, we can first obtain a consistent estimator
\mathscr{\widehat{A}}
by the methods in Sections
3
and
4
, i.e.,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textrm{SN}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textrm{MN}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textrm{SSN}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textrm{TSSN}}
, or
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textrm{NC}}
, and then apply the HOSVD to
\mathscr{\widehat{A}}
to obtain the corresponding unique estimates
\mathscr{\widehat{G}}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}
’s. That is,
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}
is calculated as the top
r_{i}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)}
with the first nonzero element in each column being positive, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}^{\top}
.

Furthermore, it is worth noting that the column space of
\mathbf{U}_{i}
is unique and identifiable, although
\mathbf{U}_{i}
suffers from rotational indeterminacy; this is similar to the loading matrix in factor models.
Thus, we can treat
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{O}
to be equivalent for any orthogonal rotation
\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{i}\times r_{i}}
, as they correspond to the same factor interpretation. Moreover, for the orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}
,
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}^{\top}
is the projection matrix of its column space. This projection matrix is unique and identifiable as
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}^{\top}=(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{O})(\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{O})^{\top}
for any orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}
. Hence, in practice, we can use the unique projection matrix
\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}^{\top}
to interpret the estimated low-dimensional factor loadings; see the empirical analysis in Section
5.2
.

2.4
Multi-Dimensional Dynamic Factor Interpretations

To illustrate the interpretation of the proposed LRTAR model, we consider the monthly import-export data among 22 countries for 15 product categories studied in
Chen et al., 2022
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22\times 15}
is the observed Export-Import-Product tensor in month
t
, with the
(i,j,k)
-th entry of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}
corresponding to the export of product
k
from country
i
to country
j
; see Section
5.2
for a detailed analysis of this dataset.

For simplicity, we first consider the proposed model with
d=2
for the data obtained by aggregating all 15 product categories, denoted
\mathbf{Y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22}
, where each row represents an exporting country and each column represents an importing country. In this case,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{4}\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22\times 22\times 22}
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times r_{i}}
, with
r_{i}
being much smaller than 22, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{4}}
. Suppose that
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s satisfy (
15
). Then the proposed LRTAR model for the matrix-valued time series
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
implies that

\mathbf{U}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{t}\mathbf{U}_{4}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$},\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{U}_{2}\rangle+\mathbf{U}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{U}_{4}.

(17)

Note that in (
17
),

\mathbf{Y}_{t}
and
\mathbf{E}_{t}
are both projected onto a low-dimensional space via
\mathbf{U}_{3}
and
\mathbf{U}_{4}
, while
\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}
is projected onto another low-dimensional space via
\mathbf{U}_{1}
and
\mathbf{U}_{2}
. This provides a multi-dimensional dynamic factor interpretation of the import-export data as follows. According to (
17
), the dynamic of the international market is driven by the low-dimensional lagged (predictor) matrix factor
\mathbf{P}_{t}:=\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{U}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}}
, whereas the effect of the past information—encapsulated by the predictor tensor factor
\mathbf{P}_{t}
—on the present state of the market is manifested through the low-dimensional (response) matrix factor
\mathbf{R}_{t}:=\mathbf{U}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{t}\mathbf{U}_{4}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{3}\times r_{4}}
. For the predictor factor,
\mathbf{U}_{1}
and
\mathbf{U}_{2}
provide factor loadings along the directions of exporting and importing countries, respectively. Similarly,
\mathbf{U}_{3}
and
\mathbf{U}_{4}
provide those for the response factor. From a dynamical system point of view, the predictor factor
\mathbf{P}_{t}
and the response factor
\mathbf{R}_{t}
can be interpreted as the input and output of the economic system, respectively, while the core tensor
\mathscr{G}
characterizes the predictive relationship between
\mathbf{P}_{t}
and
\mathbf{R}_{t}
.

The factor interpertation also applies to the general case with
d\geq 3
. For the multi-category import-export data
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22\times 15}
with
d=3
, (
17
) is extended to

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\times_{i=4}^{6}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\times_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\times_{i=4}^{6}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top},

(18)

where
(\mathbf{U}_{1},\mathbf{U}_{2},\mathbf{U}_{3})
and
(\mathbf{U}_{4},\mathbf{U}_{5},\mathbf{U}_{6})

can be viewed as loadings of exporting countries, importing countries, and product categories for the predictor and response tensor factors, respectively.
The predictor tensor factor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{P}$}_{t}:=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\times_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{3}}
drives the dynamic of the market, and the response tensor factor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{R}$}_{t}:=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\times_{i=4}^{6}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{4}\times r_{5}\times r_{6}}
reflects the reaction of the market to the past information.

Remark 1
.

In the literature on high-dimensional VAR models, a popular dimension reduction method is to impose sparsity on coefficients; see a recent review in
Basu and Matteson, 2021
. It is especially suitable for high-dimensional data where only a small subset of the variables are correlated, which is often the case in biological applications, e.g., the discovery of gene regulatory networks
(
Shojaie et al., 2012
)
.
However, in some economic and financial applications, most variables are expected to be somewhat correlated. This will often lead to many small but nonzero coefficient estimates under sparse estimation. As a result, the estimated sparse model could be hard to interpret. Rather, when pervasive cross-sectional dependency is observed in the data, it is probably more reasonable to assume that the variables in an economic or financial system are driven by some common factors. The LRTAR model provides the supervised multi-dimensional dynamic factor interpretation, which is the key advantage of the proposed model over the sparse modeling approach.

Remark 2
.

While we focus on the lag-one tensor autoregression for simplicity, the proposed model can be readily extended to the case with a general lag order; see the discussion in Section
6
.

Remark 3
.

In Appendix
S4
of the supplementary file, we further explore the relationship between the proposed LRTAR model and the tensor factor model in
Chen et al., 2022
. Note that the latter is an unsupervised learning method and cannot be used directly for forecasting, unless an explicit dynamic structure is imposed on the latent factor process. To build a connection with our model, we adapt the tensor factor model by assuming that their latent factor process follows an autoregressive model. We can show that the proposed model is more flexible than the tensor factor model with autoregressive factors.

3
Convex Estimation via Nuclear Norm Regularization

In Sections
3.1
–
3.3
, we consider a series of convex estimation methods for the proposed model via different nuclear norm regularizations. Throughout the rest of this paper, the true value of the coefficient tensor
\mathscr{A}
is denoted by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
. While our estimation methods in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
will be developed from the exact low-Tucker-rank structure of the transition tensor, our theoretical analysis will allow
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
to be approximately low-Tucker-rank, which includes the exact low-rankness as a special case. In other words, the proposed LRTAR model will be used as a working model.

3.1
Regularization via One-Mode Matricization

In model (
12
), the exactly low-rank transition tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
is subject to the constraints
r_{i}=\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})
, for
i=1,\dots,2d
. A commonly used convex relaxation of such Tucker rank constraints is the regularization via the sum of nuclear (SN) norms of all the one-mode matricizations,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}.

(19)

The SN norm has been widely used in the literature
(
Gandy et al., 2011
;
Tomioka et al., 2011
;
Liu et al., 2013
;
Raskutti et al., 2019
)
to simultaneously encourage the low-rankness for all modes of a tensor.
This leads us to the SN norm regularized estimator

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SN}}=\underset{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}}{\argmin}\left\{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}}\right\},

(20)

where
\lambda_{\text{SN}}>0
is the tuning parameter. Note that if instead of
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}}
, only one single nuclear norm, say
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}
, is penalized, then the resulting estimator will only encourage the low-rankness for the first mode of
\mathscr{A}
, while failing to do so for all the other
2d-1
modes, and hence will be less effective than the above SN estimator.

To derive the estimation error bound for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SN}}
, we make the following assumption on the random error
\mathbf{e}_{t}=\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})
.

Assumption 2
.

Let
\mathbf{e}_{t}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
, where
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\}
is a sequence of
i.i.d.
random vectors, with
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t})=\mathbf{0}
and
\textup{var}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, and
\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}}=\textup{var}(\mathbf{e}_{t})
is a positive definite matrix. In addition, the entries
(\xi_{it})_{1\leq i\leq p}
of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
are mutually independent and
\kappa^{2}
-sub-Gaussian, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}(e^{\mu\xi_{it}})\leq e^{\kappa^{2}\mu^{2}/2}
, for any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
i=1,\dots,p
.

Assumption
2
implies that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
are
i.i.d.
, which is standard in the literature on high-dimensional time series models. It may be relaxed to the weakly dependent case through strong mixing conditions as in
Wong, 2017
.
The sub-Gaussianity condition in Assumption
2
is milder than the commonly used normality assumption in the literature
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Raskutti et al., 2019
)
. This relaxation is made possible through establishing a novel martingale-based concentration bound in the proof of the deviation bound; see Lemma
S5
in Appendix
S1.4
of the supplementary file. The covariance matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}}
captures the contemporaneous dependency in
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
, and the constant
\kappa
controls the tail heaviness of the marginal distributions.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathcal{A}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{2}]}z
be a matrix polynomial, where
\mathbb{C}
is the set of complex numbers. Let

\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})=\underset{|z|=1}{\min}\,\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)\mathcal{A}(z))
and
\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})=\underset{|z|=1}{\max}\,\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)\mathcal{A}(z))
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)
is the conjugate transpose of
\mathcal{A}(z)
. It can be shown that
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})>0
under Assumption
1
; see also
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
for more discussions on the connection between the spectral density of the VAR process and the two quantities. In addition, define the positive constants

\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}=\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})}{\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})},\quad M_{1}=\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})}{\mu^{1/2}_{\min}(\mathcal{A})},\quad\text{and}\quad M_{2}=\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})}{\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})}.

Note that our theoretical analysis does not require
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
to be fixed as the dimension grows.

In practice, it could be too stringent to assume that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
is exactly low-rank. In this section, we relax it to the following approximately low-rank assumption: We assume that all one-mode matricizations of the underlying true transition tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
belong to the set of approximately low-rank matrices, namely
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(r_{q}^{(i)};p_{i},p_{-i}p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
, where
r_{q}^{(1)},\dots,r_{q}^{(2d)}>0
are the radii for all modes,

\mathbb{B}_{q}(r;d_{1},d_{2}):=\left\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}:\sum_{i=1}^{\min(d_{1},d_{2})}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})^{q}\leq r\right\}

(21)

is the set of approximately low-rank matrices defined by the
\ell_{q}
norm of the singular values,
p_{-i}=p/p_{i}=\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{d}p_{j}
for
i=1,\dots,d
, and
p_{-i}=p_{-i+d}
for
i=d+1,\dots,2d
. For the convenience of notation, we let
0^{0}=0
. Note that when
q=0
,
\mathbb{B}_{0}(r;d_{1},d_{2})
is the set of
d_{1}
-by-
d_{2}
rank-
r
matrices. For
q>0
, the restriction on
\sum_{i=1}^{\min(d_{1},d_{2})}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})^{q}\leq r
requires that the singular values decay to zero under a polynomial rate, and it is more general than the exactly low-rank assumption.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(r_{q}^{(i)};p_{i},p_{-i}p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
and radii
r_{q}^{(i)}>0
for
i=1,\dots,2d
. If
T\gtrsim\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p+\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}p
and
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}d^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{p_{-i}p/T}
, then under Assumptions
1
and
2
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{r_{q}}\left(\frac{2d\cdot\lambda_{\textup{SN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2}

(22)

with probability at least
1-2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\exp(-Cp_{-i}p)-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
, where
r_{q}=(2d)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}r_{q}^{(i)}
is the average radius for all one-mode matricizations.

By Theorem
1
, when
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\asymp\kappa^{2}M_{1}d^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{p_{-i}p/T}
, the estimation error bound scales as

\sqrt{r_{q}}(\kappa^{4}M_{1}^{2}\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-2}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p/T)^{1/2-q/4}
; note that the factor
d
in the error bounds is canceled by the
d^{-2}
in the rate of
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}
. When
\kappa
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}
and
M_{1}
are bounded, and
q=0
, namely
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
is exactly low-rank with Tucker ranks
(r_{0}^{(1)},\dots,r_{0}^{(2d)})
, the error bound reduces to
\sqrt{r_{0}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p/T}
and it is comparable to that in
Tomioka et al., 2011
for
i.i.d.
tensor regression.

However, recent research in tensor analysis
(
Mu et al., 2014
;
Raskutti et al., 2019
, e.g.,)
shows that the SN norm regularization approach can be suboptimal. For our model, this is mainly because
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}
is an unbalanced
fat-and-short
matricization of a higher-order tensor. Specifically, an essential intermediate step in the proof of Theorem
1
is to establish the deviation bound, where we need to upper bound the operator norm of a sub-Gaussian random matrix with the same dimensions as
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}
; see Lemma
S5
in Appendix
S1.3
of the supplementary file. The order of this operator norm will be dominated by the larger of the row and column dimensions of the matrix
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{-i}p}
, and hence by the column dimension
p_{-i}p
, which eventually appears in the error bound. As a result, the imbalance of the matricization leads to the efficiency bottleneck of the SN estimator.

On the other hand, since the reduced-rank VAR model can be regarded as an overparameterization of the proposed LRTAR model, alternatively one may focus on the low-rankness of the transition matrix
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{2}]}
in the VAR representation in (
14
), and adopt the matrix nuclear (MN) estimator
(
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
)
to estimate
\mathscr{A}
,

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{MN}}=\underset{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}}{\argmin}\left\{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{1}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right\},

(23)

where
\lambda_{\text{MN}}>0
is the tuning parameter.
Note that the multi-mode matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{2}]}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{1}]}^{\top}
is a
p\times p
square matrix. Thus, the loss of efficiency due to the unbalanced matricization can be avoided, which is confirmed by the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(s_{q}^{(1)};p,p)
for some
q=[0,1)
and radius
s_{q}^{(1)}>0
. If
T\gtrsim[1+\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}]p
and
\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{p/T}
, then under Assumptions
1
and
2
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{s_{q}^{(1)}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\textup{MN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2}

with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cp)-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
.

Theorem
2
shows that, with
\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\asymp\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{p/T}
, the estimation error bound for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{MN}}
scales as
\sqrt{s_{q}^{(1)}}(\kappa^{4}M_{1}^{2}\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{-2}p/T)^{1/2-q/4}
, where
s_{q}^{(1)}
is the singular value radius of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}
. This result is comparable to that in
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
for reduced-rank VAR models, yet we relax both the singular value constraint
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{1}]}^{*}\|_{\text{op}}<1
and the normality assumption on the random error in their paper. This estimation error bound is clearly smaller than that in Theorem
1
, as
(\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p/T)^{1/2-q/4}
in general can be much larger than
(p/T)^{1/2-q/4}
when
s_{q}^{(1)}\asymp r_{q}
. Therefore, adopting square matricization can indeed improve the estimation performance.

The idea of using square matricization to improve efficiency was adopted by
Mu et al., 2014
in low-rank tensor completion problems. Their proposed method, called the square deal, is to first unfold a general higher-order tensor into a matrix with similar numbers of rows and columns, and then use the MN norm as the regularizer. However, despite the advantage of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
over
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SN}}
, Theorem
2
reveals another drawback of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
.
That is, the error bounds for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
will increase as the radius
s_{q}^{(1)}
for the singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}
becomes larger.
In other words, unless we have prior knowledge that the
\ell_{q}
-“norm” of singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}
is truly small,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
may not be desirable in practice.

On the other hand, although the SN regularizer in (
19
) suffers from inefficiency due to the imbalance of one-mode matricizations, it has the attractive feature of simultaneously encouraging low-rankness across all modes of
\mathscr{A}
, and thus is more efficient than its counterpart which considers only one single one-mode matricization, say,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}
. Similarly, if we can encourage low-rankness across all possible square matricizations of
\mathscr{A}
, the estimation performance may be further improved upon
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
. This motivates us to propose a new regularization approach in the next subsection.

Remark 4
.

Since our statistical theory is non-asymptotic, the dimensions
p_{i}
’s, approximate or exact Tucker ranks such as
r_{q}^{(i)}
’s in Theorem
1
, and any other quantities appearing in the error bounds are all allowed to diverge to infinity.
Our results show how these quantities explicitly affect the error bounds. However, for simplicity of understanding the convergence rates, one may assume that
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are fixed; see Table
1
. For example, it is common to assume that
0<c\leq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\leq C
. In addition, when the spectral radius of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{2}]}
is bounded away from one, it can be shown that
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
is also bounded away from zero.

3.2
Regularization via Square Matricization

Motivated by the discussion at the end of Section
3.1
, we propose a novel convex regularizer which improves upon both SN and MN regularizers in (
19
) and (
23
), by simultaneously encouraging low-rankness across all possible square matricizations of
\mathscr{A}
.

For any
2d
-th-order tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}\times p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
, its multi-mode matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I]}
will be a
p\times p
square matrix, with
p=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
, if the index set is chosen as

I=\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{d}\},

where each index
\ell_{i}
is set to either
i
or
d+i
, for
i=1,\dots,d
. For instance,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{1}]}
is the square matricization formed by setting
\ell_{i}=i
for all
i=1,\dots,d
. Moreover, if
\mathscr{A}
has Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
, then the rank of the matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I]}
is at most
\min(\prod_{i=1,i\in I}^{2d}r_{i},\prod_{i=1,i\notin I}^{2d}r_{i})
. Therefore, if we penalize the sum of nuclear norms of all such squares matricizations, which we call the sum of square-matrix nuclear (SSN) norms for simplicity, then the resulting estimator would enjoy the efficiency gain from both the use of square matricizations and simultaneous incorporation of many rank constraints.

Obviously, there are
2^{d}
possible choices of the index set
I
that corresponds to a square matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I]}
. However, since
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I]}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I^{\complement}]}^{\top}
, when defining the SSN norm, we only need to include one of
I
and its complement
I^{\complement}
. A simple way to do so is to choose only sets containing the index one. That is, fix
\ell_{1}=1
and choose
\ell_{i}=i
or
d+i
for
i=2,\dots,d
. This results in totally
2^{d-1}
chosen index sets, denoted by
I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{2^{d-1}}
. Note that
I_{1}=S_{1}=\{1,\dots,d\}
. For example, when
d=3
, we have four chosen index sets,
I_{1}=\{1,2,3\},I_{2}=\{1,5,3\},I_{3}=\{1,2,6\}
and
I_{4}=\{1,5,6\}
.

Based on the above choice of the
2^{d-1}
index sets, we introduce the following SSN norm,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}=\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}\right\|_{\textup{nuc}}.

(24)

For a tuning parameter
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}>0
, the corresponding estimator is defined as

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}=\underset{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}}{\argmin}\left\{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}\right\}.

(25)

If the rank of one-mode matricizations
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})=r_{i}
, each square matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}
is also low-rank with
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]})\leq\min(\prod_{i=1,i\in I_{k}}^{2d}r_{i},\prod_{i=1,i\notin I_{k}}^{2d}r_{i})
. Similarly, if all
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}
s are approximately low-rank, the square matricizations are approximately low-rank as well. In contrast to the SN norm in (
19
) which directly matches the Tucker ranks
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})
for
i=1,\dots,d
, the SSN norm encourages the low-Tucker-rank structure of
\mathscr{A}
by simultaneously encouraging low-rankness of all square matricizations
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}
’s.
The following theorem gives the theoretical results for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}
.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(s_{q}^{(k)};p,p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
and radii
s_{q}^{(k)}>0
for
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
. If
T\gtrsim[1+\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}]p
and
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
,
under Assumptions
1
and
2
, then with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(p-d)]-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{s_{q}}\left(\frac{2^{d-1}\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2}

(26)

where
s_{q}=2^{1-d}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}
is the average radius for all square matricizations.

SN

MN

SSN

Sample size

T\gtrsim(\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}+M_{2}^{-2})p

T\gtrsim(1+M_{2}^{-2})p

T\gtrsim(1+M_{2}^{-2})p

Estimation error

\sqrt{r_{q}}(\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p/T)^{1/2-q/4}

\sqrt{s_{q}^{(1)}}(p/T)^{1/2-q/4}

\sqrt{s_{q}}(p/T)^{1/2-q/4}

Table 1:
Summary of sample size conditions and error bounds in Theorems
1
–
3
, where
p_{-i}=\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{d}p_{j}
,
r_{q}=(2d)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}r_{q}^{(i)}
, and
s_{q}=2^{1-d}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}
, assuming that
\kappa
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}
and
M_{2}
are bounded.

By Theorem
3
, when
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\asymp\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, the estimation error bound scales as
\sqrt{s_{q}}(\kappa^{4}M_{1}^{2}\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{-2}p/T)^{1/2-q/4}
, and reduces to
\sqrt{s_{0}p/T}
in the exactly low-rank setting for
q=0
when
\kappa
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}
and
M_{2}
are bounded. For a clearer comparison among the three estimators
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SN}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
, we summarize the main results of Theorems
1
–
3
in Table
1
.
First, both
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
have much smaller error bounds and less stringent sample size requirements than
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SN}}
, due to the diverging dimension
p_{-i}
in the results of the latter. This reaffirms the advantage of the square matricizations.

Secondly, comparing
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
to
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
, since the factor
s_{q}
in the error bounds of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
is the average of all
s_{q}^{(k)}
for
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
can protect us from the bad scenarios where the
\ell_{q}
-“norm” of the singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}
is relatively large. If all the
s_{q}^{(k)}
’s are of the same order, then the error upper bounds for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
in Table
1
will be similar. However, our simulation results in Section
5.1
show that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
clearly outperforms
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
under various settings, even when
s_{q}^{(1)}=\cdots=s_{q}^{(2d)}
.
Indeed, the error bounds for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
in Theorem
3
is likely to be loose, which is believed to be caused by taking the upper bounds on the dual norm of the SSN norm in the proof of Lemma
S3
; see Appendix
S1.3
of the supplementary file for details. By contrast, the error bounds for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
are minimax-optimal
(
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
)
.
Therefore, although our theoretical results are not sharp enough to distinguish clearly between the error rates of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
, we conjecture that the actual rate of the former is generally smaller than that of the latter. Methodologically, this is also easy to understand because, unlike
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}
simultaneously encourages the low-rankness across all square matricizations of
\mathscr{A}
rather than just on
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{1}]}
.

Remark 5
.

While our SSN regularization is proposed in the time series context, the idea of imposing joint penalties on all (close to) square matricizations of the coefficient tensor may be extended to general higher-order tensor estimation problems. It can also be refined to accommodate particular structures of the data. For example,
if some of the
d
modes of the tensor-value time series
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}
, namely
p_{1},\dots,p_{d}
, are equal, then even a greater number of possible square matricizations of
\mathscr{A}
can be formed, resulting in improved estimation efficiency.

3.3
Truncated Regularized Estimation

While the estimation methods in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
do not require exact low-rankness of the true transition tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
, sometimes imposing exact low-rankness is more desirable if one wants to interpret the underlying dynamic tensor factor structures. As discussed in Section
2.4
, the Tucker ranks determine the dimensions of the dynamic factors. For greater model interpretability, we further consider the case that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
is exactly low-rank and propose a truncation method to consistently estimate its true Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
.

Let
\gamma>0
be a threshold parameter to be chosen properly. Given the estimator
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}
, for each
i=1,\dots,2d
, we calculate the singular value decomposition (SVD) of the mode-
i
matricization
(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}})_{(i)}
with the singular values arranged in descending order. Next we truncate the SVD by retaining only singular values greater than
\gamma
, and take their corresponding left singular vectors to define the matrix
\mathbf{\widetilde{U}}_{i}
. Then, the truncated core tensor is defined as

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{\widetilde{U}}_{i}^{\top},

based on which we propose the truncated sum of square-matrix nuclear (TSSN) estimator

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{TSSN}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{\widetilde{U}}_{i}.

To derive the theoretical results on rank selection, we make the following assumption on the exact Tucker ranks and the magnitude of the singular values.

Assumption 3
.

For all
i=1,\dots,2d
,
\sigma_{r}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}^{*})=0
for all
r>r_{i}
, and there exists a constant
C>1
such that
\min_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\sigma_{r_{i}}\left(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}^{*}\right)\geq C\gamma
. As
T\rightarrow\infty
, the threshold parameter satisfies
\gamma\gg(\kappa^{2}M_{1}/\alpha_{\textup{RSC}})\sqrt{s_{0}p/T}
, where
s_{0}=2^{1-d}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\textup{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})
.

Assumption
3
requires that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
has exact Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
which do not diverge too fast. The smallest positive singular value for each
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}^{*}
is assumed to be bounded away from the threshold
\gamma
when the sample size is sufficiently large. Since Assumption
3
involves unknown quantities, it cannot be used directly for determining
\gamma
in practice. Instead, we recommend using a data-driven threshold parameter
\gamma
to be described below.

The rank selection consistency of the truncation method and the asymptotic estimation error rate of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{TSSN}}
are given by the following theorem.

Theorem 4
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3
and Assumption
3
, if the tuning parameter
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\asymp\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, then

\mathbb{P}\left\{\textup{rank}\left((\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{TSSN}})_{(i)}\right)=\textup{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}),~\text{for}~i=1,\dots,2d\right\}\to 1,

as
T\to\infty
, and for any fixed
d
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{TSSN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{s_{0}p/T}\right),

where
s_{0}
is defined as in Assumption
3
.

Similar to
Gandy et al., 2011
, the SSN estimation can be solved by the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) algorithm, while the truncation can be done by the standard HOSVD; see Appendix
S3
of the supplementary file for details. For the tuning parameter selection, since the cross-validation method is unsuitable for time series or intrinsically ordered data, we apply the Bayesian information criterion (BIC) to select the optimal
\lambda_{\text{SSN}}
, where the number of degrees of freedom is defined as
2^{-(d-1)}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{k}(2p-s_{k})
. For the threshold parameter
\gamma
of the truncated estimator, we recommend
\gamma=2^{d-1}\lambda_{\text{SSN}}/4
to practitioners, where
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}
is the optimal tuning parameter selected by the BIC. Similarly, the BIC can be used for tuning parameter selection for SN and MN estimators as well.

Remark 6
.

The Tucker ranks of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
must satisfy
r_{\max}^{2}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{2d}r_{i}
, where
r_{\max}=\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}r_{i}
; see also the discussion below (
8
). In practice, if the ranks selected by the truncated estimator fail to satisfy this condition, that is, when
\widehat{r}_{\max}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\widehat{r}_{i}
exceeds the product of the other ranks (i.e.,
\prod_{i=1,\widehat{r}_{i}\neq\widehat{r}_{\max}}^{2d}\widehat{r}_{i}
), where
\widehat{r}_{i}=\textup{rank}((\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{TSSN}})_{(i)})
, we recommend the following selection procedure for rank adjustments. First, for each
\widehat{r}_{i}
that is not equal to
\widehat{r}_{\max}
, we increase it until the above condition on Tucker ranks is met, while fixing the other ranks, and obtain the adjusted truncated estimator. Next, for all adjusted estimators, we select the most suitable ranks via BIC. For example, if the TSSN estimator produces the Tucker ranks
(3,2,1,1,1,1)
, we consider adjusted ranks
(3,3,1,1,1,1)
,
(3,2,2,1,1,1)
,
(3,2,1,2,1,1)
,
(3,2,1,1,2,1)
or
(3,2,1,1,1,2)
, and then select the one with the smallest BIC.

4
Non-convex Tensor Regression Estimation

4.1
Non-convex Estimation

While significant efficiency improvement can be achieved by the square matricization in Section
3.2
, the consistency of the SSN and TSSN estimators still requires the sample size
T
grows faster than the overall dimension
p=\prod_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}
. To further lower the sample size requirement and improve the estimation efficiency, this section proposes a non-convex estimation method for the LRTAR model under the assumption that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
is exactly low-rank.

First, we assume that the true Tucker ranks
(r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{2d})
are known.
Following
Han et al., 2022
, we can estimate the transition tensor via the non-convex (NC) optimization:

\begin{split}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}&=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}\\
&=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{2d}}\\
\mathbf{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times r_{i}}\end{subarray}}{\arg\min}\left\{\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\frac{a}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\right\},\end{split}

(27)

where the regularization terms
\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
are used to prevent
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s from being singular and balance the scale of tensor decomposition components, and
a,b>0
are tuning parameters.

To further understand the regularization terms for
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s, let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}):=(2T)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
and
\mathcal{L}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$},\mathbf{U}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{2d}):=\overline{\mathcal{L}}([\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$};\mathbf{U}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{2d}]\!])
be the squared loss functions with respect to
\mathscr{A}
and its Tucker decomposition, respectively. While the optimization in (
27
) is unconstrained, any solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
will satisfy
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}=b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}
. Otherwise, we can always find some nonsingular matrices
\mathbf{Q}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{i}\times r_{i}}
, for
i=1,\dots,2d
, such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
and
\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{i}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{i}=b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}
. In this case,
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}},\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2d})=\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{-1},\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{2d})
, while the regularization terms for
\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
reduce to zero. This will result in a contradiction with the definition of minimizers.
Note that we do not require
b=1
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
may not be orthonormal. Moreover, we do not require the uniqueness of
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
’s, since we only need the resulting
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}
from (
27
); see also
Han et al., 2022
. However, as discussed in Section
2.3
, after obtaining
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}
, we can apply the HOSVD to
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{NC}}
to obtain the uniquely defined orthonormal estimates
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{i}
’s and all-orthogonal estimate
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}
.

The partial gradients of the squared loss
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
\mathbf{U}_{i}
and
\mathscr{G}
are defined as

\nabla_{\mathbf{U}_{i}}\mathcal{L}=\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{(i)}(\otimes_{j=1,j\neq i}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{j})\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}^{\top}=\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{(i)}[\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{j=1,j\neq i}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{j}]_{(i)}~~\text{and}~~\nabla_{\scalebox{0.75}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}}\mathcal{L}=\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top},

(28)

where
\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ[\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}]
, and
\circ
denotes the tensor outer product. The problem in (
27
) can be solved by the gradient descent algorithm: for
s=0,\dots,I-1
,

\begin{split}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s+1)}&=\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{U}_{i}}\mathcal{L}^{(s)}-\eta a\left[\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}(\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}})\right],\quad i=1,\dots,2d,\\
\text{and}~\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s+1)}&=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)}-\eta\nabla_{\scalebox{0.75}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}}\mathcal{L}^{(s)},\end{split}

(29)

with the initial values
(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(0)},\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(0)},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{2d}^{(0)})
, where
I
is the total number of iterations and
\eta>0
is the step size of each iteration. The final output is
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}=[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(I)};\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(I)},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{2d}^{(I)}]\!]
, and we may apply the HOSVD to
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}
to ensure the identifiability of the Tucker decomposition.

4.2
Computational and Statistical Convergence Analysis

In this subsection, we present the main properties of the NC estimation method. Theoretical analysis of this method is challenging due to the non-convex nature of the problem. To show that the proposed method is valid, we derive the linear convergences of gradient descent iterates to the ground truth up to a statistical error. First, we introduce some regulatory conditions, namely the restricted strong convexity, restricted strong smoothness, and deviation bound conditions.

Definition 1
.

The squared loss function
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
is restricted strongly convex with parameter
\alpha
and restricted strongly smooth with parameter
\beta
, such that for any low-rank tensors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
with Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
,

\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\leq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1})-\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2})-\langle\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}),\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\rangle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}.

(30)

Definition 2
.

For the given Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
, denote
\xi(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
as

\xi(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})=\sup_{\|\scalebox{0.75}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}\|_{\textup{F}}=1,\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}}\left\langle\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}),[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$};\mathbf{U}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{2d}]\!]\right\rangle.

(31)

The restricted strong convexity and smoothness consitions are essential for convergence analysis of a large number of non-convex optimization problems
(
Jain and Kar, 2017
)
. The deviation bound
\xi(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
characterizes the magnitude of the statistical noise projected onto the low-rank tensor spaces. Moreover, for the true value
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
, denote by
\overline{\sigma}=\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\sigma_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})
,
\underline{\sigma}=\min_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\sigma_{r_{i}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})
, and
\rho=\overline{\sigma}/\underline{\sigma}
the largest singular value, the smallest nonzero singular value, and condition number of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
along all modes, respectively. Now, we state a deterministic upper bound on the estimation error and a linear rate of convergence for the proposed gradient descent algorithm.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that the squared loss function
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies the restricted strong convexity, restricted strong smoothness and deviation bound conditions in Definitions
1
and
2
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
is low-rank with known Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})
. For the gradient descent iterates with parameters
a\asymp\alpha\rho^{-2}\overline{\sigma}
,
b\asymp\overline{\sigma}^{1/(2d+1)}
, and size step
\eta=\eta_{0}\beta^{-1}\rho^{-2}\overline{\sigma}^{(-4d)/(2d+1)}
from some small
\eta_{0}>0
, if the initial bound
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\underline{\sigma}
is satisfied, for all
i=1,2\dots,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(i)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\lesssim\rho^{2}(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1}\rho^{-2})^{i}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}+\rho^{2}\alpha^{-2}\xi^{2}(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d}),

(32)

with
0<C\eta_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1}\rho^{-2}<1
.

Theorem
5
presents a set of conditions for the convergence of the gradient descent iteratives for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{NC}}
. The first term in the right hand side of (
32
) corresponds to the optimization error, whereas the second term corresponds to the statistical error. This bound shows that the estimation error of the iterates decreases exponentially to a statistical limit. When the RSC parameter
\alpha
and all nonzero singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
along all modes are bounded and bounded away from zero, the rates of paramters
a,b,\eta
remain constant.

Remark 7
.

For the initialization, if
\underline{\sigma}
is a constant number, the initial bound can be satisfied for any consistent intial value
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}
. When
p_{i}
’s are large,
\underline{\sigma}
may diverge to infinity as
p_{i}
’s increase, and hence the initial condition for
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}
could be relaxed.

Following the spectral dependency measure in Section
3
, we define the restricted strong smoothness parameter for the tensor AR process

\beta_{\text{RSS}}=[3\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})]/[2\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})]
.
For the tensor AR process satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
, we can derive the following statistical convergence results of the gradient descent iteratives.

Theorem 6
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
2
and conditions in Theorem
5
with
\alpha=\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta=\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
, if
T\gtrsim\max(\kappa^{4},\kappa^{2})M_{2}^{-2}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{i}
, when

I\gtrsim\log(\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-2}T^{-1}\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}(p_{i}r_{i})\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}^{-2})/\log(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\beta_{\textup{RSS}}^{-1}\rho^{-2})

(33)

with probability at least
1-2\exp[-CM_{2}^{2}\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})T]-C\exp(-C\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{i})
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{NC}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\rho\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{2d}p_{i}r_{i}+\prod_{i=1}^{2d}r_{i}}{T}}.

(34)

Theorem
6
presents the estimation error upper bound after a sufficient number of iterations. When
\rho
,
\kappa
,
\alpha^{-1}_{\textup{RSC}}
and
M_{1}
are bounded, the statistical convergence rate scales as
O_{p}(\sqrt{(\sum_{i=1}^{2d}p_{i}r_{i}+\prod_{i=1}^{2d}r_{i})/T})
. Under the exact low-rank condition in
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
, compared with the SSN estimator with a rate of
O_{p}(\sqrt{s_{0}p/T})
, the rate of the non-convex NC estimator is improved significantly. In other words, to achieve consistency, the sample size requirement is reduced from
T\gtrsim p=\prod_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
to
T\gtrsim\max_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}
. For high-dimensional matrix-valued (
d=2
) and tensor-valued (
d\geq 3
) time series data, the relaxation of sample size requirement is essential, since it is usually difficult or even impossible to collect a large number of samples, when
p
is large as in the import-export network data discussed in Section
1
.

4.3
Rank Selection and Initialization

In practice, we need to determine the Tucker ranks in order to apply the proposed non-convex estimation method. When the sample size is sufficiently large, i.e.,
T\gtrsim p
, one may apply the TSSN method described in Section
3.3
to select the ranks. When the dimensions
p_{i}
