’s are large, we recommend giving a pre-specified upper bound
\bar{r}_{i}>r_{i}
, and then calculate the estimate
\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}
based on the rank upper bounds
\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2},\dots,\bar{r}_{2d}
. Denote the singular values of its mode-
i
matricization by
\widetilde{\sigma}_{i1}\geq\widetilde{\sigma}_{i2}\geq\cdots\geq\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,r_{i}}
, and each rank
r_{i}
can be selected by the ridge-type ratio estimator
(
Wang et al., 2022
)

\widehat{r}_{i}=\argmin_{1\leq j\leq\bar{r}_{i}-1}\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,j+1}+s(p_{\max},T)}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{ij}+s(p_{\max},T)},

(35)

where
s(p_{\max},T)
is a positive sequence depending on
p_{\max}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{i}
and
T
.

The proposed method is not sensitive to the choice of
\bar{r}_{i}
as long as it is greater than
r_{i}
. Thus, by the multidimensional factor interpretation, we can choose
\bar{r}_{i}
to be reasonably large. For example, for the import-export network data described in Section
2.4
, we may set
\bar{r}_{1}=\cdots=\bar{r}_{6}=5
. The ridge parameter
s(p_{\max},T)
is essential for consistent rank selection, and we suggest using
s(p_{\max},T)=\sqrt{p_{\max}\log(T)/(10T)}
based on the simulation experiments in Section
5
.
Similar to the TSSN estimator, the Tucker ranks selected by the ridge-type ratio estimator may not satisfy condition (
8
) and we may adjust the selected ranks by the approach in Remark
6
.
The rank selection consistency is established in the following theorem.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that all conditions in Theorem
6
hold,
T\gtrsim\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}p_{\max}
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{p_{\max}\bar{r}_{\max}/T}\ll s(p_{\max},T)
,
s(p_{\max},T)\ll\underline{\sigma}^{-1}\min_{1\leq i\leq 2d,1\leq j\leq r_{i}-1}\sigma_{j+1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})/\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})
, and
r_{i}\leq\bar{r}_{i}
, where
\bar{r}_{\max}=\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\bar{r}_{i}
. Then
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{r}_{i}=r_{i})\to 1
, for
i=1,\dots,2d
, as
T\to\infty
.

The conditions in this theorem reduce to
s^{-1}(p_{\max},T)\sqrt{p_{\max}/T}\to 0
and
s(p,T)\to 0
as
T\to\infty
, when
\overline{\sigma}
,
\underline{\sigma}^{-1}
,
\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{-1}
,
\kappa
and
M_{1}
are bounded. Thus, the sample size requirement is reduced to
T\gtrsim p_{\max}
, which significantly relaxes that in Theorem
4
for the TSSN method.

Moreover, for the initialization of the proposed estimation methodology, we may first select the rank upper bounds
\bar{r}_{i}
and randomly initialize the algorithm by adding a random perturbation to
\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}
obtained under the rank upper bounds. The refined tensor ranks are selected by the ridge-type ratio estimator, and then the HOSVD is applied to the previous initial value to obtain
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(0)}
. The satisfactory performance of this initialization procedure is observed in our simulation experiments.

5
Numerical Studies

In this section, we present numerical studies to support the methodological and theoretical results obtained in the previous sections. In Section
5.1
, we present the finite-sample performance of various estimation methods proposed in Sections
3
and
4
. In Section
5.2
, we model the import-export network data via the LRTAR and other vector-valued and tensor-valued time series models in the literature.

5.1
Simulation Experiments

We present two simulation experiments to examine the finite-sample performance of the proposed high-dimensional estimation methods. Throughout this section, we generate the data from model (
12
) with
\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{p})
. The entries of
\mathscr{G}
are generated independently from
N(0,1)
and rescaled such that
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\|_{\text{F}}=5
. The matrices
\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\prime}s
are generated by extracting the leading singular vectors from Gaussian random matrices while ensuring the stationarity condition in Assumption
1
. In these two experiments, we consider four cases of data generating processes. In cases (a) and (b), we consider
d=2
and Tucker ranks
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})=(1,1,1,1)
,
(2,2,1,1)
or
(2,2,2,2)
; while in cases (c) and (d), we consider
d=3
and Tucker ranks
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4},r_{5},r_{6})=(1,1,1,1,1,1)
,
(2,2,2,1,1,1)
or
(2,2,2,2,2,2)
. Both pairs of cases differ in the setting for
p_{i}
’s: (a)
p_{1}=p_{2}=10
and (b)
p_{1}=p_{2}=20
; (c)
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=7
and (d)
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=15
.

The first experiment aims to compare the performance of four nuclear-norm-penalized estimators discussed in Section
3
, namely the SN, MN, SSN and TSSN estimators, when the sample size is relatively large. For each setting, we repeat 500 times and conduct the estimation using SN, MN, SSN, and TSSN. The nuclear-norm-penality tuning parameter and truncation parameter are selected by the BIC described in Section
3.3
. In Figure
2
, the average estimation errors are plotted against
T\in\{800,1000,1200,1400\}
for cases (a) and (b), and
T\in\{1000,1200,1400,1600\}
for cases (c) and (d). First, it can be seen that the SN estimator is much inferior to the other three estimators, which is due to its use of the unbalanced one-mode matricizations. Secondly, the SSN and TSSN estimators outperform the MN estimator in all cases, and their advantage is remarkably clear even when
r_{1}=\cdots=r_{2d}
. In addition, the rank selection performance of the TSSN method is summarized in Table
2
. In general, the TSSN estimator can consistently select the tensor ranks when
T
is large, and performs the best among these four, probably because it yields a more parsimonious model which improves the estimation efficiency. The results in experiment 1 verify the efficiency improvement in the proposed SSN and TSSN estimators.

The second experiment aims to verify the performance of NC estimator when the sample size is relatively small. We consider
T\in(50,100,150,200)
for cases (a) and (b), and
T\in(80,160,240,320)
for cases (c) and (d). Since the NC estimator requires the pre-determined Tucker ranks, we consider two estimators, namely the NC estimator with the true Tucker ranks (denoted by NC-true) and NC estimator with Tucker ranks estimated by the ridge-type ratio estimator in Section
4
(denoted by NC-est). When applying the gradient descent algorithm, we simply set
a=b=1
and use the TSSN estimator to obtain the initial values of
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s. The default gradient descent step size is
\eta=10^{-4}
, and it will be reduced to
10^{-5}
if the default one fails to converge. In addition, the random initialization method is also adopted for the NC-est estimator. The average estimation errors of the non-convex methods are summarized in Figure
3
and the rank determination of the ridge-type ratio estimator is collected in Table
3
. As the ridge-type ratio estimator can consistently estimate the Tucker ranks, the performance of NC-true and NC-est estimators is quite similar. When the sample size is small, NC-true method performs slightly better.

5.2
Real Data Analysis

We analyze the multi-category import-export network data in
Chen et al., 2022
, which consists of the monthly export data among 22 countries, including 19 European countries (Belgium, Bulgaria, Denmark, Finland, France, Germany, Greece, Hungary, Iceland, Ireland, Italy, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Spain, Sweden, Switzerland, Turkey, and the United Kingdom) and 3 North American countries (Canada, Mexico, and the United States). The products are classified into 15 categories, including industrial and algricultural products. Hence, the import-export network data in each month form a
22\times 22\times 15
Export-Import-Product tensor, and the data is collected from January 2010 to December 2016. Following
Chen et al., 2022
, the missing diagonal values for the export from any country to itself are treated as zero. A three-month moving average of the series is applied to alleviate the possible effect of incidental transactions, so the total available sample size is
T=84
which is much smaller than the overall dimension of the data
p=22\times 22\times 15=7,260
.

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22\times 15}
be the tensor-valued time series and denote
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})
. For comparison, we consider the following seven candidate models:

1.

The proposed LRTAR model:
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
, with
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{6}\mathbf{U}_{i}
. The model is estimated using the SSN, TSSN and NC methods, respectively.

2.

Sparse vector autoregression (SVAR):
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t}+\mathbf{e}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{7260\times 7260}
is a sparse matrix. We estimate the sparse VAR model via the Lasso estimator discussed in
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
.

3.

Low-rank vector autoregression (LRVAR):
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t}+\mathbf{e}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{7260\times 7260}
is a low-rank matrix. The model is estimated by the MN estimator in Section
3
.

4.

Vector factor model (VFM):
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\mathbf{e}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{f}_{t}
is the low-dimensional vector-valued latent factor, and
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is the loading matrix. The model is estimated by the method in
Lam et al., 2012
, and for prediction, the estimated factors
\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}
are then fitted by a VAR(1) model.

5.

Multilinear tensor autoregression (MTAR):
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\times_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{B}_{i}+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{22\times 22}
and
\mathbf{B}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{15\times 15}
are coefficient matrices. The model is estimated by the iterative least squares method similar to
Chen et al., 2021
.

6.

Tensor factor model (TFM):
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{F}$}_{t}\times_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{U}_{i}+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{F}$}_{t}
is the low-dimensional tensor-valued latent factor, and
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s are the loading matrices. The TFM is esimated by the method in
Chen et al., 2022
, and for prediction, the estimated factors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{F}}$}_{t}
are fitted by a VAR(1) model.

7.

Factor augmented vector autoregressive model (FAVAR): the vectorized time series is decomposed into two parts
\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{y}_{1t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{2t}^{\top})^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{y}_{1t}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
contains the trading data between the United States and Germany under the categories of the largest volume “Machinery and Electrical” and “Transportation”, and
\mathbf{y}_{2t}\in\mathbb{R}^{7256}
contains the rest of data. The FAVAR model
(
Bernanke et al., 2005
;
Stock and Watson, 2016
)
with
(\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{1t}^{\top})^{\top}=\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{f}_{t-1}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{1,t-1}^{\top})^{\top}+\mathbf{w}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2t}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{y}_{1t}+\mathbf{g}_{t}
is used to model the data.

We first focus on the results of the proposed LRTAR model. The overall dimension
p=7260
is much larger than the sample size
T=82
, which violates the sample size requirements of nuclear-norm-regularized estimators. Hence, we try all combinations of Tucker ranks, with each rank ranging from 1 to 3, and the best ranks selected by the BIC are
(1,1,2,2,2,2)
. By the multi-dimensional dynamic factor interpretation in (
18
), these six ranks indicate the numbers of factors for “export predictor”, “import predictor”, “product predictor”, “export response”, “import response”, and “product response.” In other words, the total number of factors for predictors (
1\times 1\times 2=2
) is smaller than that for responses (
2\times 2\times 2=8
), showing that the low-dimensional information summarized from predictors is more compact than that of responses. It is also interesting to see that the numbers of factors for predictors and responses selected by LRTAR are smaller than those selected by the tensor factor model,
(4,4,6)
, in
Chen et al., 2022
.

As the factor matrices
\mathbf{U}_{i}
’s are not uniquely defined, we present the estimates of the identifiable projection matrices
\mathbf{U}_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}
by LRTAR-NC with
\mathbf{U}_{i}
being orthonormal in Figure
4
. The estimated projection matrices of these six factor loadings offer a clear and interesting interpretation of inter-regional trading flow, which helps us answer the four questions in Section
1
.
For the first two questions about the driving forces of the exporting and importing activities, the estimated factor matrices
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{2}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{4}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{5}
present some numerical hints.
Specifically, for the responses of export and import (first two plots in the left panel of Figure
4
), the exporting countries are clearly classified into two geographical factors, one for European countries and one for North American countries, while the import countries are categorized into another two factors, United States factor and Germany factor.
For the predictors, the factor loadings for exporting and importing countries (see the first two plots in the right panel of Figure
4
) are both dominated by the United States. In other words, to forecast the trading volume in Europe and North America, the historical trading data of the United States, in both import and export, are most predictive. However, the future import and export value have a clear geographical grouping pattern.

In addition, for the third question, the factor loadings for product category,
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{3}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{6}
, also have a clear grouping pattern. For both responses and predictors, the product categories can be classified into two factors, “heavy industry factor” (mineral, chemical, machinery, electrical and transportation products) and “agricultural and light industry factor” (animal, vegetable, leather, wood, textiles products). Hence, we may interpret the estimated factor matrices in LRTAR as variable grouping patterns in export, import, and product categories for responses and predictors, respectively.
Finally, by comparing the predictor and response factor loadings, we observe that the geographical grouping patterns of both exporting and importing countries are significantly different between past and present states (i.e., predictor vs. response), whereas the grouping patterns of product categories remain almost the same.

Next, we compare the forecasting performance of seven candidate models through both average in-sample and out-of-sample forecasting errors. The average in-sample forecasting error is calculated based on the fitted models for the entire data, while the average out-of-sample forecasting error is calculated based on the rolling forecast procedure as follows. From January 2015 (
t=59
) to December 2016 (
t=84)
, we fit the models using all the available data until time
t
and obtain the one-step-ahead forecast
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{Y}}$}_{t}
. Then, we obtain the average of the rolling forecasting errors, excluding the missing diagonal entries. The number of parameters in each candidate model (LRTAR-SSN is excluded because it produces shrinkage of singular values instead of exactly low-rank structure) and the average in-sample and out-of-sample forecasting errors are summarized in Table
4
.

Model

LRTAR

SVAR

LRVAR

Table 4:
Number of parameters (No. of par.), average in-sample (IS) forecasting error and out-of-sample (OOS) rolling forecasting error for the import-export network data by various models and methods. The best cases are marked in bold.

As shown in Table
4
, all vector time series models have smaller in-sample forecasting errors and larger out-of-sample forecasting errors than their tensor counterparts, as they fail to utilize the multi-dimensional structure of the tensor data. For out-of-sample forecasting, LRTAR-NC significantly outperforms the other models in terms of average and maximum errors, as this model is much more parsimonious and can prevent overfitting effectively.

Figure 4:
NC estimates of response and predictor factor projection matrices
\mathbf{U}_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}
.

6
Conclusion and Discussion

Efficient modeling and forecasting of high-dimensional tensor time series data is an important and emerging research topic. This paper makes the first thorough attempt to address this problem from the perspective of autoregressive modeling. By assuming the exact or approximately low-Tucker-rank structure of the transition tensor, the model exploits the low-dimensional tensor dynamic structure of the high-dimensional time series data, and summarizes the complex temporal dependencies into interpretable dynamic factors.

Under the high-dimensional setting, we investigate two estimation approaches, nuclear-norm-regularized methods and non-convex methods. For the former, based on the special structure of the transition tensor, a novel convex regularizer, the SSN, is proposed, gaining efficiencies from both the square matricization and simultaneous penalization across modes. For the latter, an integrated computational and statistical analysis is provided for the gradient descent algorithm. The nuclear-norm-regularized estimators can handle the general case with approximate low-rankness, and the non-convex estimator gains efficiency improvement under the exactly low-rank setting.

We discuss several directions for future research. First, in addition to the low-rank models, sparse plus low-rank models
(
Basu et al., 2019
;
Miao et al., 2023
)
have been extensively studied in the literature of high-dimensional vector autoregression. It is also of interest to extend the proposed model in this direction, i.e., the parameter tensor
\mathscr{A}
can be decomposed into two components, the low-rank component
\mathscr{L}
and sparse component
\mathscr{S}
. Specically,
\mathscr{L}
is low-Tucker-rank as we discuss in this paper, and
\mathscr{S}
can capture the additional sparse autoregressive relationship between responses and predictors.

Second, while this paper focuses on the pure autoregressive model, the fundamental idea of leveraging the tensor-valued data and imposing the low-Tucker-rank assumption for dimension reduction can be extended to more complex settings. Similar to panel data models, exogenous variables can be further added into the regression, resulting in LRTAR-X models. For example, for the multi-category import-export data in Section
5.2
, it is possible to consider

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}_{t}+\langle\mathbf{B},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{t}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
, where the vector
\mathbf{x}_{t}
may contain global variables such as the return of the oil price, and the matrix or tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{t}
may contain other country-level macroeconomic indicators such as the GDP growth rate.
When the dimensions of
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{t}
are high, a low-dimensional structure, such as sparsity, group sparsity or low-rankness, can be imposed on
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
\mathscr{B}
to improve the estimation efficiency.

Third, in the proposed model, all variables in
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}
are treated with equal importance because the primary objective is to capture the complex dependence structures of a global system using granular data. However, if there are other priority variables to forecast, represented by the vector
\mathbf{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{x}}
, then we may extend the proposed method to the joint model,

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}

\displaystyle=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{y},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{y}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}_{t}+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{x},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{x}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}_{t-1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t},

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{x}\times p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
can be assumed to have low Tucker ranks.

Fourth, the proposed methods can be generalized to the LRTAR model of finite lag order
L
, defined as

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle+\cdots+\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{L},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-L}\rangle+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
,
where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1},\dots,\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{L}
are
2d
-th-order Tucker low-rank coefficient tensors. Then, one may consider the SSN regularized estimation by minimizing

T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{L}\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{j},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-j}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{L}\lambda_{j}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{j}\|_{\text{SSN}}
.
In addition,
the NC estimator can be defined as the minimizer of
(2T)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+(a/2)\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
, which can be implemented by the gradient descent algorithm.

Finally, heavy-tailed distributions and outliers are commonly observed in empirical economic and financial datasets. Robust estimation methods against the heavy-tailed distribution for high-dimensional VAR models have been investigated recently
(
Liu and Zhang, 2022
;
Wang and Tsay, 2022
)
, and it is of practical importance to investigate the robust methods for the proposed model.

Supplementary Material for

“High-Dimensional Low-Rank Tensor Autoregressive Time Series Modeling”

S1
Proofs for Convex Regularized Estimation

In this appendix, we provide the proofs of Theorems
1
–
4
in Section
3
. We start with a preliminary analysis in Appendix
S1.1
which lays out the common technical framework for proving the estimation and prediction error bounds of the SN, MN and SSN regularized estimators, and four lemmas, Lemmas
S1
–
S4
, are introduced herein.
Then in Appendix
S1.2
we give the proofs of Theorems
1
–
4
. The proofs of Lemmas
S1
–
S4
are provided in Appendix
S1.3
, and three auxiliary lemmas are collected in Appendix
S1.4

S1.1
Preliminary Analysis

The technical framework for proving the error bounds in Theorem
1
–
3
consists of two main steps, a deterministic analysis and a stochastic analysis, given in Sections
S1.1.1
and
S1.1.2
, respectively. The goal of the first one is to derive the error bounds given the deterministic realization of the time series, assuming that the parameters satisfy certain regularity conditions. The goal of the second one is to verify that under stochasticity these regularity conditions are satisfied with high probability.

S1.1.1
Deterministic Analysis

Throughout the appendix, we adopt the following notations. We use
C
to denote a generic positive constant, which is independent of the dimensions and the sample size. For any matrix
\mathbf{M}
and a compatible subspace
\mathcal{S}
, we denote by
\mathbf{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
the projection of
\mathbf{M}
onto
\mathcal{S}
. In addition, let
\text{col}(\mathbf{M})
be the column space of
\mathbf{M}
, and let
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
be the complement of the subspace
\mathcal{S}
. For a generic tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
, the dual norms of its SSN norm and SN norm, denoted by
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}
and
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\|_{\text{SN}^{*}}
, respectively, are defined as

\begin{split}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}=\sup_{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}},\|\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}}\|_{\text{SSN}}\leq 1}\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\rangle,~~\text{and}~~\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\|_{\text{SN}^{*}}=\sup_{\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}},\|\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}}\|_{\text{SN}}\leq 1}\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\rangle.\end{split}

(S1)

Moreover, for any two tensors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{m+1}\times\cdots\times p_{m_{n}}}
, their tensor outer product is defined as
(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{m}\times p_{m+1}\times\cdots\times p_{m+n}}
where

(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$})_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}i_{m+1}\dots i_{m+n}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{i_{m+1}\dots i_{m+n}},

(S2)

for any
1\leq i_{1}\leq p_{1}
,
\dots
,
1\leq i_{m+n}\leq p_{m+n}
.

For the theory of regularized
M
-estimators, restricted error sets and restricted strong convexity are essential definitions. To define the former, we need to first introduce the following restricted model subspaces.

For
i=1,\dots,2d
, denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
the spaces spanned by the first
r_{i}
left and right singular vectors in the SVD of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}
, respectively.
Define the collections of subspaces

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{2d})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{\perp}=(\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{1}^{\perp},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{2d}^{\perp}),

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{i}&=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{-i}p}|\text{col}(\mathbf{M})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i},\text{col}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\},\\
\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{\perp}&=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{-i}p}|\text{col}(\mathbf{M})\perp\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i},\text{col}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})\perp\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\},\end{split}

(S3)

for
i=1,\dots,2d
.
Note that
\mathcal{N}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
.

Furthermore, for
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
, denote by
\mathcal{U}_{k}
and
\mathcal{V}_{k}
the spaces spanned by the first
s_{k}^{*}
left and right singular vectors in the SVD of the square matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}
, respectively, where
s_{k}^{*}=\textup{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}^{*})
. Similarly, define the collections of subspaces

\mathcal{M}:=(\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{2^{d-1}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}=(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\perp},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2^{d-1}}^{\perp}),

where

\begin{split}&\mathcal{M}_{k}=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}|\text{col}(\mathbf{M})\subset\mathcal{U}_{k},~\text{col}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})\subset\mathcal{V}_{k}\},\\
&\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k}^{\perp}=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}|\text{col}(\mathbf{M})\perp\mathcal{U}_{k},~\text{col}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})\perp\mathcal{V}_{k}\},\end{split}

(S4)

for
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
. In particular, as described in Section
3.2
,
I_{1}=S_{1}=\{1,\dots,d\}
. Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\perp}
are the subspaces associated with the square matricization
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}
.

Then, for simplicity, for any
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
, we denote

\begin{split}&\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\mathcal{N}}^{(i)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{(i)})_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}^{(i)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{(i)})_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\perp}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}}^{(i)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{(i)})_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{i}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{\perp}}^{(i)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{(i)})_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{i}^{\perp}}\\
&\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\mathcal{M}}^{(k)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{[I_{k}]})_{\mathcal{M}_{k}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}^{(k)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{[I_{k}]})_{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\perp}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{[I_{k}]})_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k}},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}_{[I_{k}]})_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k}^{\perp}},\end{split}

(S5)

where
i=1,\dots,2d
and
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
. Based on the subspaces defined in (
S3
) and (
S4
), we can define the restricted error sets corresponding to the three regularized estimators as follows.

Definition 3
.

The restricted error set corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is defined as

\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}):=\left\{\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}:\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\leq 3\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(k)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+4\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right\}.

(S6)

The restricted error set corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{N}}
is defined as

\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SN}}(\overline{\mathcal{N}}):=\left\{\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}:\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{\perp}}^{(i)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\leq 3\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(i)}_{\overline{\mathcal{N}}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+4\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(i)}_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right\}.

(S7)

The restricted error set corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
is defined as

\mathbb{C}_{\textup{MN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}):=\left\{\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}:\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\leq 3\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(1)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+4\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(1)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right\}.

(S8)

The first lemma shows that if the tuning parameter is well chosen for each regularized estimator, the estimation error belongs to the corresponding restricted error set.

Lemma S1
.

For the SSN estimator, if the regularization parameter
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\textup{SSN}^{*}}
, the error
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{SSN}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SSN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}
belongs to the set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
.

For the SN estimator, if the regularization parameter
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\textup{SN}^{*}}
, the error
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{SN}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{SN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}
belongs to the set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SN}}(\overline{\mathcal{N}})
.

For the MN estimator, if the regularization parameter
\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})^{\top}\|_{\textup{nuc}}
, the error
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{MN}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\textup{MN}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}
belongs to the set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{MN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{(1)})
.

Following
Negahban and Wainwright, 2012
and
Negahban et al., 2012
, a restricted strong convexity (RSC) condition for the square loss function can be defined as follows.

Definition 4
.

The loss function satisfies the RSC condition with curvature
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}>0
and restricted error set
\mathbb{C}
, if

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\geq\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2},~~~\forall\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{C}.

(S9)

Based on the restricted error sets and RSC conditions, the estimation errors have the following deterministic upper bounds.

Lemma S2
.

Suppose that
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\textup{SSN}^{*}}
, the RSC condition holds with the parameter
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and restricted error set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(s_{q}^{(k)};p,p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
and all
k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}
,

\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{SSN}}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{s_{q}}\left(\frac{2^{d-1}\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2},

(S10)

where
s_{q}=2^{1-d}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}
.

Suppose that
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\textup{SN}^{*}}
, the RSC condition holds with the parameter
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and restricted error set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{SN}}(\overline{\mathcal{N}})
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(r_{q}^{(i)};p_{i},p_{-i}p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
and all
i=1,\dots,2d
,

\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{SN}}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{r_{q}}\left(\frac{2d\cdot\lambda_{\textup{SN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2},

(S11)

where
r_{q}=(2d)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}r_{q}^{(i)}
.

Suppose that
\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})\|_{\textup{nuc}}
, the RSC condition holds with the parameter
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and restricted error set
\mathbb{C}_{\textup{MN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1})
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{1}]}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}(s_{q}^{(1)};p,p)
for some
q\in[0,1)
,

\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\textup{MN}}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{s_{q}^{(1)}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\textup{MN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2}.

(S12)

Note that Lemma
S2
is deterministic and the radius
s_{q}
,
r_{q}
, and
s_{q}^{(1)}
can also diverge to infinity.

S1.1.2
Stochastic Analysis

We continue with the stochastic analysis to show that the deviation bound and the RSC condition hold simultaneously with high probability.

Lemma S3

(Deviation bound)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. If
T\gtrsim p
and
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(p-d)]
,

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\textup{SSN}^{*}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}}{4}

(S13)

where
M_{1}=\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})/\mu^{1/2}_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
.

If
T\gtrsim\max_{1\leq i\leq d}p_{-i}p
and
\lambda_{\textup{SN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}d^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{p_{-i}p/T}
, with probability at least
1-2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\exp(-Cp_{-i}p)
,

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\textup{SN}^{*}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\textup{SN}}}{4}.

(S14)

Moreover, if
T\gtrsim p
and
\lambda_{\textup{MN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{p/T}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cp)
,

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})^{\top}\right\|_{\textup{op}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\textup{MN}}}{4}.

(S15)

Next, we prove the restricted strong convexity for regularized estimators. According to Lemma
S3
, we need the sample size
T\gtrsim p
for all three estimators. In this case, we can establish the strong convexity condition that is stronger than the RSC condition.

Lemma S4

(Strong convexity)
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, for
T\gtrsim\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}p
, with probability at least
1-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
,

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\geq\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2},

(S16)

where
M_{2}=[\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})]/[\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})]
and
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})/(2\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A}))
.

S1.2
Proofs of Theorems
1
–
4

Proof of Theorems
1
and
2
.

Theorems
1
and
2
can be proved based on Lemmas
S2
–
S4
following the same line of the proof of Theorem
3
given below. Therefore, we omit the details here. ∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

The proof of Theorem
3
has been split into Lemmas
S2
–
S4
. By Lemma
S2
, for deterministic realization with sample size
T
of a tensor autoregressive process, if we choose
\lambda_{\text{SSN}}\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}
and RSC condition holds for the square loss with the parameter
\alpha_{\text{RSC}}
, the following error upper bound can be established

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sqrt{s_{q}}\left(\frac{2^{d-1}\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}}{\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}}\right)^{1-q/2}.

(S17)

Denote the events
E_{1}(\beta)=\{\beta\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}\}
and
E_{2}(\alpha)=\{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T)\geq\alpha\}
.
If we take
\lambda_{\text{SSN}}\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, it suffices to show that
E_{1}(C\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T})
and
E_{2}(\alpha_{\text{RSC}}/2)
occur simultaneously with high probability.

By Lemma
S3
, when
T\gtrsim p
,

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}\lesssim\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{\frac{p}{T}}

(S18)

with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(p-d)]
.

By Lemma
S4
, when
T\gtrsim\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}p
, for any
\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
,

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})}{2\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

(S19)

with probability at least
1-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
.

Hence, when
T\gtrsim[1+\max(\kappa^{2},\kappa^{4})M_{2}^{-2}]p
and
\lambda\gtrsim\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(p-d)]-\exp[-C\min(\kappa^{-2},\kappa^{-4})M_{2}^{2}p]
, the condition
\lambda\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}
and the RSC condition with the parameter
\alpha_{\text{RSC}}=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})/\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})
hold.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Theorem
3
gives the Frobenius estimation error bound. For simplicity, we write
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{TSSN}}
in this proof.
By definition, for any tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}),~~i=1,2,\dots,2d.

(S20)

In other words, the Frobenius norm of the error tensor is equivalent to the
\ell_{2}
norm of singular values of the one-mode matricization. By Mirsky’s singular value inequality
(
Mirsky, 1960
)
,

\sum_{j=1}^{p_{i}}[\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})-\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})]^{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p_{i}}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})=\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2},~~i=1,2,\dots,2d.

(S21)

Obviously, the
\ell_{\infty}
error bound is smaller than the
\ell_{2}
error bound, so it follows the same upper bound. By Theorem
3
, when
\lambda_{\textup{SSN}}\asymp\kappa^{2}M_{1}2^{1-d}\sqrt{p/T}
, with probability approaching one,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\max_{1\leq j\leq p_{i}}|\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})-\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})|\leq

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{p_{i}}[\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})-\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})]^{2}\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}\lesssim\frac{\kappa^{2}M_{1}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}}\sqrt{\frac{s_{0}p}{T}}.

(S22)

Therefore, by Assumption
3
, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\gamma\gg\max_{1\leq i\leq 2d}\max_{1\leq j\leq p_{i}}|\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})-\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})|.

(S23)

Then, for any
j>r_{i}
, since
\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})=0
, we have
\gamma\gg\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})
. Thus, for all
i=1,\dots,2d
,
\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})
will be truncated for all
j>r_{i}
.
Meanwhile, by Assumption
3
and (
S22
), we have
\sigma_{r_{i}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(i)})>\gamma
for
T
sufficiently large, for all
i=1,\dots,2d
. Therefore, the rank selection consistency of the truncated estimator
\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}
can be established.

Denote the event
E=\{\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}_{(i)})=r_{i},~\text{for}~i=1,\dots,2d\}
. For a generic tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}}
, denote the sub-tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{k}=j}
, a
p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{k-1}\times 1\times p_{k+1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}
tensor such that

(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{k}=j})_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}1i_{k+1}\dots i_{2d}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}ji_{k+1}\dots i_{2d}},

(S24)

and sub-tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{k}>j}
, a
p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{k-1}\times(p_{k}-j)\times p_{k+1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}
tensor such that

(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{k}>j})_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}\ell i_{k+1}\dots i_{2d}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}(\ell+j)i_{k+1}\dots i_{2d}}.

(S25)

Let the HOSVD of
\mathscr{\widehat{A}}
be
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}\times_{i=1}^{2d}\mathbf{\widehat{U}}_{i}
. By definition,
\mathscr{\widehat{G}}
is a
p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{2d}
all-orthogonal and sorted tensor such that

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}=1}\|_{\text{F}}\geq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}=2}\|_{\text{F}}\geq\cdots\geq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}=p_{k}}\|_{\text{F}},

(S26)

for
k=1,\dots,2d
. On
E
, the truncation procedure is equivalent to truncating all the sub-tensors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}>r_{k}}
to zeros. Thus,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}\|_{\text{F}}=\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}>r_{k}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
.

By the definition of HOSVD,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}=j}\|_{\text{F}}=\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{(k)})=\sigma_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(k)})
, and then

\begin{split}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{i_{k}>r_{k}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\sum_{i=r_{k+1}}^{p_{k}}\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(k)})&=\sum_{i=r_{k+1}}^{p_{k}}[\sigma_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(k)})-\sigma_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(k)})]^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p_{k}}[\sigma_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{(k)})-\sigma_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(k)})]^{2}\leq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2},\end{split}

(S27)

where the last inequality follows from (
S21
).

Finally, on the event
E
,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}\leq\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}+\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}\leq(1+\sqrt{2d})\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}
, where
d
is fixed. Note that Theorem
3
implies the asymptotic rate
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{s_{0}p/T})
and the first part of this proof shows that
\mathbb{P}(E)\to 1
, as
T\to\infty
. The proof is complete. ∎

S1.3
Proofs of Lemmas
S1
–
S4

Proof of Lemma
S1
.

In this part, we focus on
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SSN}}
and simplify it to
\mathscr{\widehat{A}}
. The tuning parameter
\lambda_{\text{SSN}}
is simplified to
\lambda
. The proof can be readily extended to
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{SN}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{\text{MN}}
.

Note that the quadratic loss function can be rewritten as
\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$})=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t}-\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}
, where
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t})
. By the optimality of the SSN estimator,

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}\\
\Rightarrow~&\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\mathbf{e}_{t},\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\rangle+\lambda(\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}})\\
\Rightarrow~&\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left\langle T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t},\mathbf{\Delta}\right\rangle+\lambda(\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}})\\
\Rightarrow~&\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{SSN}}\left|\left|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right|\right|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}+\lambda(\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}),\end{split}

(S28)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}
refers to the dual norm of the SSN norm.

By triangle inequality and decomposability, we have

\begin{split}&\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}=\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}+\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}=\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\\
=&\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{\mathcal{M}}^{*(k)}+\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}^{*(k)}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\\
\geq&\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left[\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{\mathcal{M}}^{*(k)}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}^{*(k)}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right]\\
\geq&\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left[\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-2\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}^{*(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right].\end{split}

(S29)

If
\lambda\geq 4\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}
, we have

\begin{split}0&\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\lambda(\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}})\\
&\leq\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left[\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(k)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(k)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-2\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(k)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+4\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+2\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right]\\
&=\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left[3\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+4\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}-\|\mathbf{\Delta}^{(k)}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right].\end{split}

(S30)

Hence, the error
\mathbf{\Delta}
lies in the restricted error set
\mathbb{C}_{\text{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
S2
.

Similar to Lemma
S1
, we focus on the SSN estimator, and the results for SN and MN estimators can be extended in a similar way.

Note that
T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}
.
Following the proof of Lemma
S1
,
\mathbf{\Delta}\in\mathbb{C}_{\text{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
and

\begin{split}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}&\leq\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{SSN}}+\lambda(\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\text{SSN}}-\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}\|_{\text{SSN}})\leq\frac{3\lambda}{2}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{SSN}}\\
&=\frac{3\lambda}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\leq\frac{3\lambda}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left(\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\perp}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\right)\\
&\leq 6\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\textup{nuc}}+6\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\\
&\leq 6\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\sqrt{2s_{k}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}\|_{\text{F}}+6\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\\
&\lesssim\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\sqrt{2s_{k}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}+6\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}\end{split}

(S31)

where the last inequality stems from the fact that
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{(k)}
has a matrix rank at most
2s_{k}
, similar to Lemma 1 in
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
.

As the RSC condition holds with the parameter
\alpha_{\text{RSC}}
and restricted error set
\mathbb{C}_{\text{SSN}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
,

\alpha_{\text{RSC}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\langle\mathbf{\Delta},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\rangle\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\lesssim\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\sqrt{s_{k}}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}+\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\text{nuc}}.

(S32)

Thus, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\lambda^{2}(\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\sqrt{s_{k}})^{2}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{2}}+\frac{\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}}\lesssim\frac{\lambda^{2}2^{d-1}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{k}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{2}}+\frac{\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}}.\end{split}

(S33)

Consider any threhold
\tau_{k}\geq 0
and define the thresholded subspace
\mathcal{M}^{(k)}
corresponding to the column and row spaces spanned by the first
r^{(k)}
singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}
where
\sigma_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{r^{(k)}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})>\tau_{k}\geq\sigma_{r^{(k)}+1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})
.
By the definition of
\mathbb{B}_{q}(s_{q}^{(k)};p,p)
, we have
s_{q}^{(k)}\geq r^{(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}^{q}
and thus
r^{(k)}\leq s_{q}^{(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}^{-q}
.

Then, the approximation error can be bounded by

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*(k)}_{\mathcal{M}^{\perp}}\|_{\textup{nuc}}=\sum_{r=r^{(k)}+1}^{p}\sigma_{r}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})=\sum_{r=r^{(k)}+1}^{p}\sigma_{r}^{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})\cdot\sigma_{r}^{1-q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{[I_{k}]})\leq s_{q}^{(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}^{1-q}.

(S34)

The estimation error can be bounded by

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\lambda^{2}2^{d-1}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}^{-q}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{2}}+\frac{\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}^{1-q}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}}.

(S35)

Setting each
\tau_{k}\asymp\alpha_{\text{RSC}}^{-1}(q/(1-q))2^{d-1}\lambda
, the upper bound can be minimized to

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\lesssim 2^{1-d}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}s_{q}^{(k)}\left(\frac{\lambda\cdot 2^{d-1}}{\alpha_{\text{RSC}}}\right)^{2-q}.

(S36)

The proof is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
S3
.

First, we derive an upper bound of the dual norm of the SSN norm. By definition, for any tensor
\mathscr{A}
and collection of index sets
\mathbb{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{2^{d-1}}\}
, the SSN norm is

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}=\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\textup{nuc}},

(S37)

and its dual norm is
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}:=\sup\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\rangle
such that
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{W}$}\|_{\text{SSN}}\leq 1
. By a method similar to that in
Tomioka et al., 2011
, it can be shown that

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}=\inf_{\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}}_{k}=\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}}\max_{k=1,\dots,2^{d-1}}\|(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{k})_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\text{op}}.

(S38)

Then, we can take
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{k}=(\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}1/c_{k})^{-1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}/c_{k})
, where
c_{k}=\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\text{op}}
, and apply Jensen’s inequality so that we have

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}\leq 2^{-2(d-1)}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{[I_{k}]}\|_{\text{op}}.

(S39)

Hence, we have

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{2(d-1)}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d-1}}\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I_{k}]}\right\|_{\text{op}}.

(S40)

In other words, the dual norm of the SSN norm can be upper bounded by the sum of the scaled matrix operator norms of different matricizations of the tensor
T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}
.

All of the square matricizations based on
I_{k}
lead to a square
p
-by-
p
matrix. Therefore, by the deviation bound in Lemma
S5
, we can take a union bound such that

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\text{SSN}^{*}}\leq\frac{C\kappa^{2}M_{1}}{2^{d-1}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{T}}

(S41)

with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(p-d)]
.

Next, for the SN estimator, we can obtain a similar upper bound of the dual norm of the SN norm. The SN norm is defined as

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}\|_{\textup{nuc}},

(S42)

and its dual norm has the equivalent form

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}^{*}}=\inf_{\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}}_{i}=\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}}\max_{i=1,\dots,2d}\|(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{i})_{(i)}\|_{\text{op}}.

(S43)

Then, we can obtain an upper bound,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\|_{\text{SN}^{*}}\leq\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)}\|_{\text{op}}.

(S44)

Then, for each one-mode matricization, we have the deviation bound. Then, we can take a union bound such that

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t}\right\|_{\text{SN}^{*}}\leq\frac{C\kappa^{2}M_{1}}{(2d)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\sqrt{\frac{p_{-i}p}{T}},

(S45)

with probability at least
1-2d\exp[-Cp]
.

Finally, the MN estimator uses a special case of square matricization, and the upper bound for the MN estimator can be obtained by Lemma
S5
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
S4
.

For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}
, denote
R_{T}(\mathbf{M})=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
. Note that
R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})\geq\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})-\sup_{\mathbf{\Delta}}|R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})|
. Following the proof of Lemma
S5
,
\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})=\|(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})\mathbf{PD}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\geq T\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\cdot\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})
.

Similar to Lemma
S6
, for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
and any
t>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})|\geq t]\\
\leq&2\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}T\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})}\right)\right).\end{split}

(S46)

Considering an
\epsilon
-covering net of
\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
, by Lemma
S7
, we can easily construct the union bound for
T\gtrsim p
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}}|R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})|\geq t\right]\\
\leq&C\exp\left(p-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}T\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})}\right)\right),\end{split}

(S47)

Letting
t=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})/2
, for
T\gtrsim M_{2}^{-2}\max(\kappa^{4},\kappa^{2})p
, we have

\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{v}^{\top})|\geq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})/2]\leq 2\exp(-CM_{2}^{2}\min(\kappa^{-4},\kappa^{-2})T),

(S48)

where
M_{2}=[\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})]/[\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})]
.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-CM_{2}^{2}\min(\kappa^{-4},\kappa^{-2})T)
,

R_{T}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{[S_{2}]})\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}.

(S49)

Finally, since
\mathbf{P}
is related to the VMA(
\infty
) process, by the spectral measure of ARMA process discussed in
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
, we may replace
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
and
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
with
1/\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
and
1/\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})
, respectively.
∎

S1.4
Three Auxiliary Lemmas

Three auxiliary lemmas used in the proofs of Lemmas
S3
and
S4
are presented below.

Lemma S5

(Deviation bound on different matricizations)
.

For any index set
I\subset\{1,2,\dots,2d\}
, denote
q=\prod_{i=1,i\in I}^{2d}p_{i}
and
q^{\prime}=\prod_{i=1,i\notin I}^{2d}p_{i}
. If
T\gtrsim(q+q^{\prime})
, with probability at least
1-\exp[-C(q+q^{\prime})]
,

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]}\right\|_{\textup{op}}<C\kappa^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{(q+q^{\prime})/T}.

(S50)

where
M_{1}=\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})/\mu^{1/2}_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

For any index set
I\subset\{1,2,\dots,2d\}
and
2d
th-mode tensor
\mathscr{T}
, denote the inverse operation of the multi-mode matricization
\mathbf{T}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{[I]}
by
\mathbf{T}^{[I]}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}
. Denote
\mathcal{W}(r;q,q^{\prime})=\{\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q^{\prime}}:\textup{rank}(\mathbf{W})=r,~\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\textup{F}}=1\}
.

By definition,
\|T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]}\|_{\textup{op}}=\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\rangle=\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})\textup{vec}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1})^{\top},(\mathbf{W}^{[I]})_{[S_{1}]}^{\top}\rangle
.

For an arbitrary matrix
\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q^{\prime}}
such that
\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\textup{F}}=1
, denote
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{W}^{[I]})_{[S_{1}]}^{\top}
. Then, one can easily check that
\langle(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{e}_{t},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\rangle
.

For a fixed
\mathbf{M}
, denote
S_{t}(\mathbf{M})=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\langle\mathbf{e}_{s},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{s-1}\rangle
and
R_{t}(\mathbf{M})=\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{s}\|_{2}^{2}
, for
1\leq t\leq T
. By the standard Chernoff argument, for any
\alpha>0
,
\beta>0
and
c>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[\{S_{T}(\mathbf{M})\geq\alpha\}\cap\{R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta\}]\\
=&\inf_{m>0}\mathbb{P}[\{\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M}))\geq\exp(m\alpha)\}\cap\{R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta\}]\\
=&\inf_{m>0}\mathbb{P}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M}))\mathbb{I}(R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta)\geq\exp(m\alpha)]\\
\leq&\inf_{m>0}\exp(-m\alpha)\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M}))\mathbb{I}(R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta)]\\
=&\inf_{m>0}\exp(-m\alpha+cm^{2}\beta)\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M})-cm^{2}\beta)\mathbb{I}(R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta)]\\
\leq&\inf_{m>0}\exp(-m\alpha+cm^{2}\beta)\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M})-cm^{2}R_{T}(\mathbf{M}))].\end{split}

(S51)

By the tower rule, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M})-cm^{2}R_{T}(\mathbf{M}))]\\
=&\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M})-cm^{2}R_{T}(\mathbf{M}))]|\mathcal{F}_{T-1}]\\
=&\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T-1}(\mathbf{M})-cm^{2}R_{T-1}(\mathbf{M}))\mathbb{E}[\exp(m\langle\mathbf{e}_{T},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\rangle-cm^{2}\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T}\|_{2}^{2})|\mathcal{F}_{T-1}]].\end{split}

(S52)

Since
\langle\mathbf{e}_{T},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{\xi}_{T},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}^{1/2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\rangle
, one can easily check that
\langle\mathbf{e}_{T},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\rangle
is a
\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\|_{2}^{2}
-sub-Gaussian random variable. In other words,
\mathbb{E}[\exp(m\langle\mathbf{e}_{T},\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\rangle)]\leq\exp(m^{2}\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\|_{2}^{2}/2)
.
Thus, letting
c=\kappa\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})/2
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T}(\mathbf{M})-m^{2}\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})R_{T}(\mathbf{M})/2)]\\
\leq&\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{T-1}(\mathbf{M})-m^{2}\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})R_{T-1}(\mathbf{M})/2)]\\
\leq&\cdots\leq\mathbb{E}[\exp(mS_{1}(\mathbf{M})-m^{2}\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})R_{1}(\mathbf{M})/2)]\leq 1.\end{split}

(S53)

Hence, we have that, for any
\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[\{S_{T}(\mathbf{M})\geq\alpha\}\cap\{R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq\beta\}]\\
\leq&\inf_{m>0}\exp(-m\alpha+m^{2}\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\beta/2)\\
=&\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\beta}\right).\end{split}

(S54)

By Lemma
S6
, we have that for any
t>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{M})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{M})|\geq t]\\
\leq&2\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}T\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})}\right)\right).\end{split}

(S55)

In addition,
\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{M})=\text{tr}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}})=\|(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\mathbf{M})\mathbf{P}\mathbf{D}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\leq T\cdot\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
. Letting
t=C\kappa^{2}T\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
, we have

\mathbb{P}[R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\geq C\kappa^{2}T\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})]\leq 2\exp(-CT).

(S56)

Next, consider a
\epsilon
-net
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})
for
\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})
. For any matrix
\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})
, there exist a matrix
\overline{\mathbf{W}}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})
such that
\|\mathbf{W}-\overline{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\textup{F}}\leq\epsilon
. Since the rank of
\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}=\mathbf{W}-\overline{\mathbf{W}}
is at most 2, we can split the SVD of
\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}
into 2 parts, such that
\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}=\mathbf{\Delta}_{1}+\mathbf{\Delta}_{2}
, where
\textup{rank}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{1})=\textup{rank}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{2})=1
and
\langle\mathbf{\Delta}_{1},\mathbf{\Delta}_{2}\rangle=0
. Then, for any matrix
\mathbf{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q^{\prime}}
, we have

\langle\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{N},\overline{\mathbf{W}}\rangle+\langle\mathbf{N},\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{N},\overline{\mathbf{W}}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\langle\mathbf{N},\mathbf{\Delta}_{i}/\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{i}\|_{\textup{F}}\rangle\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{i}\|_{\textup{F}},

(S57)

where
\mathbf{\Delta}_{i}/\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{i}\|_{\textup{F}}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})
. Since
\|\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}=\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{1}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}+\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{2}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}
, by Cauchy inequality,
\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{1}\|_{\textup{F}}+\|\mathbf{\Delta}_{2}\|_{\textup{F}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}\|_{\text{F}}=\sqrt{2}\epsilon
. Hence, we have

\gamma:=\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W}\rangle\leq\max_{\overline{\mathbf{W}}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{N},\overline{\mathbf{W}}\rangle+\sqrt{2}\gamma\epsilon.

(S58)

In other words,

\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{N},\mathbf{W}\rangle\leq(1-\sqrt{2}\epsilon)^{-1}\max_{\overline{\mathbf{W}}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{N},\overline{\mathbf{W}}\rangle.

(S59)

Therefore, we have that, for any
x>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq x\right]\\
\leq&\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{\mathbf{W}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq(1-\sqrt{2}\epsilon)x\right]\\
\leq&|\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})|\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq(1-\sqrt{2}\epsilon)x\right].\end{split}

(S60)

Note that by (
S54
), for any
x>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq(1-\sqrt{2}\epsilon)x\right]\\
\leq&\mathbb{P}[\{S_{T}(\mathbf{M})\geq T(1-\sqrt{2}\epsilon)x\}\cap\{R_{T}(\mathbf{M})\leq C\kappa^{2}T\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})\}]\\
+&\mathbb{P}[R_{T}(\mathbf{M})>C\kappa^{2}T\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})]\\
\leq&\exp\left[-\frac{CTx^{2}}{\kappa^{4}\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})}\right]+2\exp(-CT).\end{split}

(S61)

By Lemma 3.1 in
Candes and Plan, 2011
, for a
\epsilon
-net for
\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})
, the covering number
|\overline{\mathcal{W}}(1;q,q^{\prime})|\leq(9/\epsilon)^{q+q^{\prime}}
. Combining (
S60
), we have that, when
T\gtrsim q+q^{\prime}
, for any
x>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq x\right]\\
\leq&\exp\left[(q+q^{\prime})\log(9/\epsilon)-\frac{CTx^{2}}{\kappa^{4}\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})}\right]+2\exp[(q+q^{\prime})\log(9/\epsilon)-CT].\end{split}

(S62)

Taking
\epsilon=0.1
and
x=C\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{1/2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})\cdot\sqrt{(q+q^{\prime})/T}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{W}(1;q,q^{\prime})}\left\langle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{Y}$}_{t-1}\circ\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{E}$}_{t})_{[I]},\mathbf{W}\right\rangle\geq C\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{1/2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})\sqrt{\frac{q+q^{\prime}}{T}}\right]\\
\leq&\exp[-C(q+q^{\prime})].\end{split}

(S63)

Finally, since
\mathbf{P}
is related to the VMA(
\infty
) process, by the spectral measure of ARMA process discussed in
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
, we may replace
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
with
1/\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
.
∎

Lemma S6
.

Suppose we simplify the notation of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
to
\mathbf{A}
. For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
such that
\|\mathbf{M}\|_{\textup{F}}=1
, denote
R_{T}(\mathbf{M})=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
. Then, for any
t>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{M})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{M})|\geq t]\\
\leq&2\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}T\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{PP}^{\top})}\right)\right),\end{split}

(S64)

where
\mathbf{P}
is defined as

\mathbf{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{p}&\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{A}^{2}&\mathbf{A}^{3}&\dots&\mathbf{A}^{T-1}&\dots\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{I}_{p}&\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{A}^{2}&\dots&\mathbf{A}^{T-2}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\dots\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\dots&\mathbf{I}_{p}&\dots\end{bmatrix}.

(S65)

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{T-1}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{T-2}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{0}^{\top})^{\top}
,
\mathbf{e}=(\mathbf{e}_{T-1}^{\top},\mathbf{e}_{T-2}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{0}^{\top},\dots)^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{\xi}=(\mathbf{\xi}_{T-1}^{\top},\mathbf{\xi}_{T-2}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{\xi}_{0}^{\top},\dots)^{\top}
. Based on the moving average representation of VAR(1), we can rewrite
\mathbf{y}_{t}
to a VMA(
\infty
),
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{e}_{t}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{t-1}+\mathbf{A}^{2}\mathbf{e}_{t-2}+\mathbf{A}^{3}\mathbf{e}_{t-2}+\cdots
. Note that
R_{T}(\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{y}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{e}^{\top}\mathbf{P}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})\mathbf{P}\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{\xi}^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{P}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})\mathbf{P}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{\xi}:=\mathbf{\xi}^{\top}\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}}\mathbf{\xi}
, where
P
is defined in (
S65
) and

\mathbf{D}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}^{1/2}&\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\dots\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}^{1/2}&\mathbf{O}&\dots\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}^{1/2}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}.

(S66)

By Hanson-Wright inequality, for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{M})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{M})|\geq t]\leq 2\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}\|\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\|\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}}\|_{\text{op}}}\right)\right).

(S67)

As
\|\mathbf{M}\|_{\text{F}}=1
, by the submultiplicative property of the Frobenius norm and operator norm, we have
\|\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\leq T\cdot\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
and
\|\mathbf{\Sigma_{M}}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})
. These imply that, for any
t>0
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[|R_{T}(\mathbf{M})-\mathbb{E}R_{T}(\mathbf{M})|\geq t]\\
\leq&2\exp\left(-\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\kappa^{4}T\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}^{2}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})},\frac{t}{\kappa^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma_{e}})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})}\right)\right).\end{split}

(S68)

The proof of this lemma is accomplished. ∎

Lemma S7
.

(Covering number of unit sphere) Let
\mathcal{N}
be an
\varepsilon
-net of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. Then,

|\mathcal{N}|\leq\left(\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\right)^{p}.

(S69)

Proof.

This lemma follows directly from Corollary 4.2.13 of
Vershynin, 2018
.
∎

S2
Proofs for Non-Convex Estimation

In this Appendix, we present the theoretical analysis for the NC estimation approach. In Appendix
S2.1
, we present the proof of Theorem
5
by providing the local convergence guarantees for the algorithm. The optimization error of the gradient descent iteratives are shown to converge linearly to zero, and the dominating term is the statistical error rate after a sufficient number of iterations. In Appendix
S2.2
, we present the statistical convergence analysis. To be specific, we prove the stochastic RSC, RSS and deviation conditions in the analysis of the local convergence analysis. In Appendix
S2.3
, we present the theoretical justification of the ridge-type ratio estimator for rank selection. Auxiliary lemmas are presented at the end of each appendix.

S2.1
Proof of Local Convergence Analysis

Proof of Theorem
5
.

The proof generally follows that of Theorem 3.1 of
Han et al., 2022
. We devide the proof into four steps. For brevity, we focus on the case with
d=3
, and the results can be readily extended to the general case of
d>3
. In Step 1, we introduce the notations and conditions. In Steps 2 and 3, we present some intermediate results. Finally, in Step 4, we present the local convergence results and verify the conditions imposed in Step 1.

Step 1 (Notations and Conditions)

We first introduce some notations used in the proof. Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}=[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*};\mathbf{U}_{1}^{*},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{6}^{*}]\!]
such that
\mathbf{U}^{*\top}\mathbf{U}_{i}^{*}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{i}}
. For each step
s=0,1,\dots,I
, we define

\begin{split}E^{(s)}&=\min_{\mathbf{R}_{i}\in\mathbb{O}_{r_{i}}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{6}\left\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}-\mathbf{U}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{i}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\left\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)}-[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{R}_{6}^{\top}]\!]\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\right\},\\
(\mathbf{R}_{1}^{(s)},\dots,\mathbf{R}_{6}^{(s)})&={\arg\min}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}\in\mathbb{O}_{r_{i}}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{6}\left\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}-\mathbf{U}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{i}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\left\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)}-[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\dots,\mathbf{R}_{6}^{\top}]\!]\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\right\},\\
\mathbf{V}_{i}^{(s)}&=\left(\otimes_{j\neq i}\mathbf{U}_{j}^{(s)}\right)\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)\top}_{(i)}~\text{and}~\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(s)}=[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)};\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(s)},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{6}^{(s)}]\!].\end{split}

(S70)

For any
s=0,1,\dots,I
, we have

\|\mathbf{U}_{i}^{(s)}\|_{\text{op}}\leq 1.01b,~\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(s)}_{(i)}\|_{\text{op}}\leq 1.01b,~\text{for}~i=1,\dots,2d,~\text{and}~E^{(s)}\leq c_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1}\rho^{-2}.

(S71)

By definition, for the given sample size
T
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
is restricted strongly convex (RSC) with parameter
\alpha
and restricted strongly smooth (RSS) with parameter
\beta
, such that for any tensors
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\dots\times p_{d}\times p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{d}}
with Tucker ranks
(r_{1},\dots,r_{2d})

\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\leq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1})-\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2})-\langle\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}),\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\rangle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{2}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}.

(S72)

The
\alpha
-RSC condition implies that
